Highly contagious: US loses 26-year measles elimination status as cases more than double

News Brief
The United States has lost its measles elimination status, with more than 5,400 confirmed cases in 2026, more than double the 2,289 cases reported in 2025. At least five measles deaths have been confirmed. Measles is highly contagious and can cause pneumonia, brain swelling, and death, with young children, pregnant women, and people with weakened immune systems at greater risk of serious complications. The CDC is committed to stopping outbreaks and regaining elimination status through vaccination efforts and resource support.
Original source text (English)
Skip to Article Set weather Back To Main Menu Close Weather in - Change Location Current -Mostly Sunny TonightLow -Mostly Sunny TomorrowHigh -Mostly Sunny Full Weather Report News Highly contagious: US loses 26-year measles elimination status as cases more than double Published: Nov. 18, 2026, 11:59 a.m. The highly contagious respiratory disease spreads through coughing and sneezing, and the virus can remain in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves.Canva Illustration for NJ.com Set as Google Preferred Source By Dallas Gagnon | MassLive.com After 26 years, the United States has officially lost its measles elimination status, meaning the virus has circulated continuously for more than a year. Measles cases have more than doubled in 2026, with 5,412 confirmed cases reported as of Nov. 12 compared with 2,289 during all of 2025. That surge has come with serious consequences. Measles is highly contagious and can cause pneumonia, brain swelling and death, with young children, pregnant women and people with weakened immune systems among those at greater risk of serious complications. At least five measles deaths have been confirmed by the CDC in 2026. Earlier this month, the Wisconsin Department of Health Servicesreported that an infant died amid an ongoing outbreak in the state, with a coroner listing measles as the immediate cause of death. Just days earlier, New York reported its first known measles-associated death in more than 30 years after a child in Lewis County died amid an ongoing outbreak. Against that backdrop, the Pan American Health Organization issued a formal letter to U.S. health officials last week declaring the country lost its status after “it was determined that a single measles chain of transmission, which commenced on 20 January 2025, has persisted for more than twelve months in the United States.” The U.S. is one of three countries that has lost its elimination status in 2026, joining Bolivia and Mexico. Read more: Medicare Part B 2027 premium announced: Here’s how much its increasing While PAHO determined that endemic measles transmission has been re-established in the U.S., elimination status for rubella and congenital rubella syndrome remains unchanged. “We encourage the United States to intensify these efforts until transmission is interrupted and the outbreak is fully controlled, honoring the commitment made by countries in the Americas in 1994 to achieve and sustain a measles-free future for the peoples of the Americas,” PAHO Director Jarbas Barbosa da Silva Jr. wrote in the letter. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention issued a statement on Nov. 17, stating the agency is “committed to stopping measles outbreaks, protecting Americans, and regaining measles elimination status.” The CDC said it will encourage MMR vaccinations, expand access to vaccines while also assisting communities and health departments with the resources needed to prevent and “respond effectively to outbreaks.” How did the U.S. lose its measles elimination status? What happened? The U.S. earned measles elimination status in 2000 following widespread vaccination and the absence of continuous virus transmission for more than 12 months. Elimination doesn’t mean a country has zero measles cases. It means there has been no continuous transmission for more than 12 months. The U.S. lost its elimination status after the Pan American Health Organization determined that a single chain of measles transmission that began Jan. 20, 2025, had persisted for more than 12 months. As of Nov. 12, cases were reported by 47 jurisdictions and 18 cases were reported among international visitors to the U.S. Read more: Can GLP-1s actually save on health care costs? CVS says yes, but only at this price There have been 43 new outbreaks reported in 2026, and 96% of confirmed cases (5,216 of 5,412) are outbreak-associated. People who were unvaccinated or whose vaccination status was unknown accounted for 96% of cases in 2026 and 93% in 2025. The resurgence comes as childhood vaccination coverage continues to decline. CDC data shows MMR coverage among U.S. kindergartners fell to 92.4% during the 2025-26 school year, leaving about 280,000 kindergartners without documentation that they had completed the vaccine series. Exemptions from one or more vaccines also increased from 3.6% to 4.2%. The Pan American Health Organization has warned that suboptimal vaccination coverage and growing immunity gaps are among the factors helping measles outbreaks persist. What happens next? The CDC says regaining elimination status will require sustained action across federal, state and local public health agencies, health care providers, laboratories and communities. The agency plans to encourage MMR vaccination, expand access to vaccines, strengthen data sharing and help health departments identify high-risk communities, detect cases and respond to outbreaks. CDC said it has provided approximately $100 million in additional funding since August for measles and other vaccine-preventable disease prevention and response efforts through most of 2027. Read more: Major SNAP rule delayed 90 days: What it means for millions of shoppers That includes $50.2 million aimed at identifying emerging risks, addressing vaccination gaps and supporting outbreak response, along with $49.8 million for outbreak prevention, preparedness, detection and response. CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz pointed to vaccinations as a crucial part of regaining elimination status. “The measles elimination designation is not what protects Americans,” she said. “Vaccination does.” More Consumer-Focused News You Can Use ‘GTA VI’ makes major change to longstanding feature Do not eat: Recalled sandwiches might cause illness Walmart’s third-quarter earnings exceeded expectations as e-commerce sales dominated Dallas Gagnon Dallas Gagnon is a Business Trending Reporter and a Reporting Fellow with the Newhouse School of Communications. U.S. Loses Measles Elimination Status After 26 Years. Here's Why - Healthline

The United States has lost its measles elimination status after 26 years, with 5,412 confirmed measles cases in 2026, the highest total in 35 years. 96% of these cases were in unvaccinated or unknown vaccination status individuals. The measles virus is highly contagious and spreads through the air, leading to ongoing circulation and person-to-person transmission in the U.S.

Find a Doctor FindCare Cardiology Dentistry Dermatology Neurology OB-GYN Orthopedic Surgery Pediatrics Primary Care Psychiatry Urology Search Healthline Subscribe Health NewsFact Checked U.S. Loses Measles-Free Status After 26 Years, a Major Public Health Setback Written by Kristen B. Reed — Fact checked by Jill Seladi-Schulman, Ph.D. — Updated on November 17, 2026 Add Healthline on Google Share on Pinterest A certified medical assistant administering the MMR vaccine to patients at the Parkside Pediatrics practice in Spartanburg, SC, on January 30, 2026. The United States has lost its measles elimination status after 26 years, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) announced on November 17. U.S. measles cases reached 5,412 in 2026, the highest total in 35 years, according to Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) data. About 96% of this year’s measles cases were in people who were unvaccinated or had unknown vaccination status. Two doses of the MMR vaccine are about 97% effective and offer the best protection against measles, according to the CDC. The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status, a major public health achievement that was secured 26 years ago. The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), the World Health Organization’s (WHO) regional office for the Americas, determined that measles has spread continuously in the U.S. for more than 12 months. On November 17, PAHO announced the end of the country’s measles elimination status, which it has held since 2000. “Losing a measles elimination status has broader implications than losing a public health designation,” PAHO’s director, Jarbas Barbosa, MD, MPH, PhD, said in a media briefing. “It means the virus has circulated for a prolonged period of time, putting families and communities at risk of a preventable illness, hospitalization, and death.” PAHO’s review examined measles spread starting January 20, 2025, when the first person became ill in a large West Texas outbreak, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC)Trusted Source. CDC dataTrusted Source show 5,412 confirmed measles cases this year as of November 12. That’s the highest annual total since 1991 and more than double the 2025 count of 2,289 cases. Andi Shane, MD, chief of pediatric infectious diseases at Children’s Healthcare of Atlanta, called the U.S. losing measles elimination status “a public health alarm that we are not being effective and we’re not controlling measles as well.” The measles virus is highly contagious. It spreads through the air when an infected person coughs or sneezes. People can catch it in a room up to two hours after an infected person has left, according to the CDCTrusted Source. What losing measles-free status means for the U.S. “There’s really been very little measles in the United States over the past 25 years until January 2025,” William Moss, MD, a professor of epidemiology at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, told Healthline. Losing the status itself doesn’t change much about daily life for most Americans, Moss said. The concern is the measles outbreaks, hospitalizations and deaths behind the loss, he said. He called the loss “a big red flag” after “enormous efforts over the past 26 years” to keep the status. A country loses its elimination status when there is “uninterrupted circulation of the same virus genotype and lineage for 12 months or more,” according to PAHO. Shane explained it this way: “For the past 12 months in the United States, we’ve had ongoing circulation of the measles virus and person-to-person transmission is occurring.” Canada lost its elimination status in November 2025, and the U.K. and five European countries lost theirs earlier in 2026. The U.S. can regain the status. “Other countries have done that,” Moss said. Brazil, which lost its status in 2019, regained it in November 2024. Venezuela, which lost its status in 2018, regained it in 2023, according to PAHO. ADVERTISEMENT One test checks immunity for 5 essential vaccines Our 5-in-1 test can confirm your immunity status for school or work requirements in one simple panel. GET TESTED Clinics 5 miles away (95% of the U.S.) HSA/FSA Payments AcceptedWalk-ins Welcome4.8 ★'s on App Store How the U.S. eliminated measles: A timeline Before the measles vaccine, measles infected an estimated 3 million to 4 million Americans each year, according to the CDCTrusted Source. Among reported cases, about 400 to 500 people died each year, 48,000 needed hospital care, and 1,000 developed encephalitis, or brain swelling. Here’s a timeline of measles elimination in the U.S.: 1954: Scientists isolate the measles virus during an outbreak in Boston (CDCTrusted Source). 1963: The U.S. licenses its first measles vaccine (CDCTrusted Source). 1968: An improved, more weakened live-virus vaccine goes into use. (CDCTrusted Source). 1971: The U.S. licenses the combined measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine (CDCTrusted Source). 1978: The CDC sets a goal to eliminate measles by 1982. The country misses the deadline, though reported cases drop 80% between 1980 and 1981 (CDCTrusted Source). 1989: After outbreaks among vaccinated school-age children, national health groups recommend a second MMR dose for all children (CDCTrusted Source). 1994: The Vaccines for Children program, created by Congress in 1993, begins providing free vaccines to eligible children (CDCTrusted Source). 2000: The U.S. declares measles eliminated (CDCTrusted Source). Jan. 20, 2025: The West Texas outbreak begins (CDCTrusted Source). Nov. 2026: PAHO rules the U.S. has lost its elimination status. Moss said elimination efforts “really intensified” after a large outbreak from 1989 to 1992. Part of that push was “really promoting school entry vaccination checks,” he said. “That was one of the key policy changes that I think allowed for measles elimination in the United States.” Every state and Washington, D.C., require children to be vaccinated against measles to attend school, though exemptions vary by state, according to the nonprofit health policy organization KFF. Why measles made a comeback Fewer kindergartners are getting the MMR vaccine, according to the CDC. Kindergarten MMR coverage fell from 95.2% in the 2019–2020 school year to 92.4% in 2025–2026. About 280,000 kindergartners started the 2025–2026 school year without records showing they had completed the MMR series, the CDC reported.Trusted Source Shane said “95 out of 100 people need to be protected against measles” for a community to get herd protection. “So since we’ve been below that for many, many months, that is also contributing to the loss of our elimination status,” she said. “It takes two things to have a measles outbreak,” Moss said. A community needs low vaccine coverage, and someone with measles has to enter it and “start the chain of transmission,” he said. About 96% of this year’s cases were among people who were unvaccinated or had unknown vaccination status, up from 93% in 2025, according to the CDCTrusted Source. Moss said many of those cases are adults. “This is particularly true in Pennsylvania, where about two-thirds of the cases are in adults,” he said. Many never got vaccinated as kids and grew up while measles was rare, so they never built natural immunity, he said. He noted that having so many unvaccinated adults puts pregnant people and adults with chronic conditions like lung disease at risk of serious illness. The U.S. has already seen “dozens of pregnant women actually getting infected during pregnancy,” he said. Infected parents can also spread measles to young children and grandparents, Shane said. Shane traced much of the hesitancy to the pandemic. “With COVID there’s been an overall distrust in vaccines and vaccination that initially started with COVID but has now spread to other vaccines,” she said. Moss pointed to the federal government. “We know what it takes to achieve and sustain elimination. We have the expertise. We have the resources,” he said. He cited staff cuts at the CDC and said messaging from Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and President Donald Trump has undermined vaccine confidence. He called federal messaging on vaccines “really mixed and weak.” Moss also criticized the President’s August 10 executive order calling for the MMR vaccine to be split into separate shots for each disease. “There’s no justification for that, and those vaccines don’t even exist in the United States,” he said. Measles vaccine offers best protection against the disease Measles may cause ear infections, diarrhea, pneumonia, encephalitis and, in some cases, death, according to the CDCTrusted Source. The disease may also weaken the immune system for weeks to a year after recovery, raising the risk of flu, COVID-19, or RSV, Shane said. “It’s not a benign childhood disease,” Moss said. The best protection against measles is two doses of the MMR vaccine, which are about 97% effective, according to the CDCTrusted Source. Shane said, “There are not very many other things we do in life that give you such great protection.” People who missed doses can still catch up, she added. “If you just don’t know or can’t tell, there’s no harm — as long as you’re eligible — in getting another MMR vaccine.” Shane added that “parents really want to do the right thing for their children.” “We know that the MMR vaccine — that’s the vaccine that provides protection against measles — is a safe vaccine. It’s a very effective vaccine,” Shane said. Babies under 12 months are too young for the vaccine. “So they depend on the protection of the people around them,” Shane said. The CDC recommends a first MMR dose at 12 to 15 months and a second at 4 to 6 years. Babies 6 to 11 months old who will travel internationally should get an early dose, and adults without proof of immunity should get at least one dose, according to the CDCTrusted Source. To stop the outbreaks, Moss said the U.S. will need to “increase vaccine coverage, particularly in those communities that have had long-standing low vaccine rates.” So far, the response has been “more reactive than proactive,” Moss said. Public health teams scramble once an outbreak starts, he said, and he wants more outreach to at-risk communities before measles arrives. “I am worried about the ability of the United States to regain measles elimination status in the current climate,” Moss said. How we reviewed this article: History Share this article Written by Kristen B. Reed — Fact checked by Jill Seladi-Schulman, Ph.D. — Updated on November 17, 2026 Was this article helpful? The loss of elimination status would be a huge disappointment for the country, which fought to immunize people and wipe out the virus in 2000. What Parents Should Know About the Long-Term Effects of the Measles Virus Despite being declared eliminated in the United States in 2000, the measles virus has made a comeback. Many people do not know it can lead to long-term health effects including brain damage, hearing loss, and immune suppression. Measles Hits Texas as Washington Outbreak Gets Worse A measles outbreak has again appeared in a U.S. region, despite the fact that the virus was declared "eliminated" by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention in 2000. As Canada Loses Measles Elimination Status, Experts Warn U.S. Could Be Next Canada has lost its measles elimination status, which means the United States could soon follow suit. Rising measles cases in 43 U.S. jurisdictions among mostly unvaccinated individuals have caused alarm among public health officials. Why This Could Be the Worst Year for Measles in a Decade As of last week, 228 measles cases have been confirmed in the United States this year, according to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

美国失去 麻疹 淘汰地位,自 1991 年以来最糟糕的 麻疹 - 卫报

US loses measles elimination status amid worst outbreak since 1991 - The Guardian
新闻简报美国已经失去了 麻疹 的消除地位,这标志着在 26 年没有 麻疹 的情况下对公共卫生造成的重大挫折。泛美卫生组织基于 麻疹 传播 在美国、墨西哥和玻利维亚重新流行的情况而作出这一决定。2026 年的 暴发 导致 5,400 多例病例和 8 人死亡,凸显了大流行时代的干扰和错误信息造成的低 疫苗接种 率的影响。公共卫生官员强调需要加强 监测、及时发现病例并有针对性地努力解决这一问题。失去消除地位引起了批评,特别是针对前卫生部长小罗伯特·F·肯尼迪,他面临传播 疫苗 错误信息的指控。正在努力缩小 疫苗接种 差距并重建公众信任,官员们正在努力防止进一步的 暴发 并及时支持 治疗。这种情况反映了 疫苗接种 发病率下降和 麻疹 病例上升的更广泛的全球趋势,强调了解决 疫苗 犹豫和错误信息以保护公众健康的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
美国于 2026 年失去了 麻疹 消除状态,并于 2026 年 11 月 17 日宣布。麻疹 自今年年初以来一直在进行,到 2026 年底报告了超过 5,400 例病例和 8 例死亡。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在美国,影响了全国各地的社区和人口,特别是那些 疫苗接种 发病率较低的社区和人群。泛美卫生组织确认 麻疹 传播 在美国、墨西哥和玻利维亚重新流行。
事件
美国失去了 麻疹 消除状态,标志着公共卫生领域的重大倒退。包括泛美卫生组织在内的全球卫生当局证实了这一点,该组织表示,麻疹 在被消灭 26 年后,目前仍在美国持续传播。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年,暴发 导致了 5,400 多例病例和 8 人死亡。由于 传播 率较低的地区,持续 传播 的风险很高,而这些地区在大流行时代的干扰和错误信息之后有所增加。消除状态的丧失引发了人们对公共卫生措施有效性以及进一步 暴发 的可能性的担忧。
应对、状态与不确定性
美国公共卫生系统一直致力于通过加强 监测、及时发现病例、有针对性的 疫苗接种 活动以及卫生部门之间的协调来管理 暴发。我们正在努力缩小 疫苗接种 差距并重建公众信任。泛美卫生组织的决定是基于证据,包括对阳性患者的访谈和病毒的整个 基因组 测序。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

US loses measles elimination status amid worst outbreak since 1991 - The Guardian

News Brief
The US has lost its measles elimination status, marking a major setback for public health after 26 years of being measles-free. This decision, made by the Pan American Health Organization, was based on the re-establishment of endemic measles transmission in the US, Mexico, and Bolivia. The outbreak in 2026 resulted in over 5,400 cases and eight deaths, highlighting the impact of low vaccination rates fueled by pandemic-era disruptions and misinformation. Public health officials have emphasized the need for strengthened surveillance, timely case detection, and targeted vaccination efforts to address the issue. The loss of elimination status has drawn criticism, particularly against former health secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr, who faced accusations of spreading vaccine misinformation. Efforts are ongoing to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust, with officials working to prevent further outbreaks and support timely treatment. The situation reflects a broader global trend of declining vaccination rates and rising measles cases, underscoring the importance of addressing vaccine hesitancy and misinformation to protect public health.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main contentSkip to navigation Support the Guardian Fund independent journalism with $7.50 per month Make a year-end gift Make a year-end gift Print subscriptions Newsletters Sign in US US edition UK edition Australia edition Europe edition International edition The Guardian - Back to homeThe Guardian News Opinion Sport Culture Lifestyle Show moreHide expanded menu News View all News US news US politics Midterms 2026 World news Climate crisis Middle East Ukraine US immigration Soccer Business Environment Tech Science Newsletters The Filter Wellness Opinion View all Opinion The Guardian view Columnists Letters Opinion videos Cartoons Sport View all Sport Soccer NFL Tennis MLB MLS NBA WNBA NHL F1 Golf Culture View all Culture Film Books Music Art & design TV & radio Stage Classical Games Design a better world Lifestyle View all Lifestyle The Filter Wellness Fashion Food Recipes Love & sex Home & garden Health & fitness Family Travel Money Search input google-search Search Support us Print subscriptions Newsletters Download the app Digital Archive Guardian Licensing Live events About Us The Guardian app Video Podcasts Pictures Inside the Guardian Guardian Weekly Crosswords Puzzles & games Corrections Tips Search input google-search Search Digital Archive Guardian Licensing Live events About Us US news US politics Midterms 2026 World Climate crisis Middle East Ukraine US immigration Soccer Business Environment Tech Science Newsletters The Filter Wellness 1:32 US loses measles elimination status, major setback for US public health – video US loses measles elimination status amid worst outbreak since 1991 Infectious disease is now continually spreading after being eliminated for 26 years, with more than 5,400 US cases The US has lost its measles elimination status, global health authorities announced, an official recognition that the infectious disease is endemic in the US after being eliminated for 26 years. The announcement comes as the US endures the worst measles outbreak since 1991, with more than 5,400 cases and eight deaths in 2026. “Measles is resurging globally amid the persistent gaps in vaccination coverage, and this global trend is also affecting the Americas,” said Dr Jarbas Barbosa da Silva Jr, the director of the Pan American Health Organization, a regional branch of the World Health Organization. “The endemic measles transmission has now been re-established in Bolivia, Mexico and United States.” Silva made the final decision on US elimination status. In a statement to the Guardian on Tuesday, Barbosa da Silva commended “the significant efforts undertaken by the United States to manage the measles outbreak through strengthened surveillance, timely case detection and response, targeted vaccination activities and coordination across the public health system”. Measles is one of the most contagious viruses known to medicine and requires 95% of the community to be vaccinated to prevent outbreaks. The measles vaccine is safe and highly effective. However, pockets of low vaccination have grown in the US following pandemic-era disruptions and widespread misinformation. Measles, mumps and misinformation: how Crunchy Moms became ‘the most dangerous’ group on Facebook Read more The nation’s largest doctors group, the American Medical Association, described loss of measles elimination status as a “deeply troubling and entirely preventable setback for our nation”. The loss of measles-free status comes after Donald Trump and his health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, spent nearly two years sowing confusion about the safety of vaccines. In just one recent example, Trump called for the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) combination vaccine to be split into separate shots, arguing without evidence that “together, there could be a possibility they’re quite lethal”. “As a pediatrician, I saw firsthand how decisions made in Washington hurt our children and families,” said Dr Annie Andrews, a Democratic senator-elect. “Nothing makes that clearer than the loss of our measles elimination status.” The loss of elimination status has already heightened criticism of Kennedy. High-profile epidemiologists called Kennedy’s work at the health department “malpractice” and equivalent to a “crime scene”. In a statement posted on X late on Tuesday afternoon, Kennedy said the US “will stop measles outbreaks, protect American families, and work to regain U.S. measles elimination status”. He added that he would “continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles” and that officials with the Department of Health & Human Services “are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust. We will answer families’ questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment.” Barbosa da Silva called the role of misinformation in measles transmission “dynamic” and urged health departments to develop bespoke campaigns tailored to undervaccinated communities. Experts caution against piling blame for measles outbreaks solely on the Trump administration. The MMR vaccine has been the target of misinformation for more than two decades, including work by Kennedy before his White House role. “There is a profound problem with measles in all three North American countries,” said Noel Brewer, a behavioral scientist at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill who studies vaccination behaviors, and specifically measles. He chairs a committee that considers evidence on US measles elimination status, and contributed to the scientific work that underpinned Pan American Health Organization’s decision. “The US has had large outbreaks and exported those to Canada and Mexico, and those cases have come back again to the US,” said Brewer. Canada lost its measles-free status in 2025, but Barbosa da Silva said the country now represented the “good news” in the region. The nation stopped measles transmission for 60 days. This is the first step on the way to regaining measles elimination status. The UK and several European nations have also recently lost elimination status. “What we’re experiencing is not a United States-specific thing,” said David Margolius of the Big Cities Health Coalition. Instead, there is a, “global surge in cases, global decline in vaccination rate”. Public health leaders with the Big Cities Health Coalition (BHC), which leads health departments that serve 47 million Americans, described the announcement as a “profoundly sad moment for our country”. “Recent declines in measles vaccination rates have opened the door to outbreaks across the country, resulting in preventable illness, hospitalizations, and tragic loss of life,” BHC members said in a statement. “These outcomes are especially heartbreaking because measles is a disease that we know how to prevent. Yet too many voices spreading fear and false information are exacerbating the problem.” Expert epidemiologists recommended US measles status change during a closed-door meeting in Washington DC. A range of evidence would have been presented, including interviews with positive patients collected by local US health authorities and whole genome sequencing that can provide a fingerprint of the virus’s origin. Although measles elimination status is an official designation, it carries real consequences for Americans. Where measles cases were once typically imported by international travelers, endemic measles transmission means Americans face new risks of infection, and are being asked to dig up decades-old vaccination certificates and possibly quarantine for 21 days. “Now is the time to go and find that evidence, because now this is a real day-to-day life thing that could happen to you,” Margolius said earlier in November. Re-establishing elimination status will require “a national plan to eliminate measles again”, said Brewer, “an integrated plan that’s publicly communicated with specific tasks assigned to named parties”. He added that the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and the president should speak out “forcefully” in favor of vaccination, and Congress should allocate more funding. 在正在进行的 麻疹 中,美国正式失去 麻疹 淘汰地位 - CNBC

U.S. officially loses measles elimination status amid ongoing outbreaks - CNBC

B routine priorityPublic-health news

U.S. officially loses measles elimination status amid ongoing outbreaks - CNBC

News Brief
The U.S. has officially lost its measles-free status, following 12 months of continuous transmission and ongoing outbreaks. This comes amid declining vaccination rates and vaccine hesitancy, leading to 5,400 cases and 10 deaths in 2026. Public health officials are ramping up vaccination efforts and surveillance to control the outbreaks and regain the measles-free status.

Backward← Captions On/Offc Fullscreen/Exit Fullscreenf Mute/Unmutem Seek %0-9 1x1.5x2x Listen6min Live 00:00 00:00 06:34 2x1.5x1x Key Points The U.S. has lost its official designation as a measles-free nation, more than 25 years after it eliminated the highly contagious virus. The director of the Pan American Health Organization made the final call that measles is endemic — or always present — in the U.S. following 12 months of continuous transmission. It’s a devastating step backward for American public health, as declining childhood vaccination rates help to fuel outbreaks that have sickened thousands and led to a string of preventable deaths. A vial of MMR vaccine is prepared at Dr. Gary M. Kramer’s pediatric office in Coral Gables, Florida, Sept. 12, 2025. Joe Raedle | Getty Images The U.S. on Tuesday lost its official designation as a measles-free nation, more than 25 years after it eliminated the highly contagious virus. The Pan American Health Organization announced the decision, which came after an independent panel of eight scientific experts convened last week to consider the country’s epidemiological evidence. The director of PAHO, the regional branch of the World Health Organization, made the final call that measles is endemic — or always present — in the U.S. following 12 months of continuous transmission. In other words, measles is spreading regularly in the country again, with ongoing outbreaks sparking new ones. “The commission concluded that endemic measles transmission has now been reestablished in Bolivia, Mexico and the United States, and recommended that their measles elimination status be withdrawn — a recommendation which I have accepted,” Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, PAHO director, said during a news briefing on Tuesday. It’s a devastating step backward for American public health, as declining childhood vaccination rates and vaccine hesitancy fuel outbreaks across the country that have sickened thousands of people and led to a tragic string of preventable deaths. Among the deaths where vaccination status was reported, all were unvaccinated. More than 5,400 measles cases had been reported nationwide in 2026 as of mid-November, marking the country’s worst outbreak in more than three decades. States have confirmed 10 deaths associated with the disease so far in 2026: seven in Pennsylvania, one in New York and two in Wisconsin. The commission found that out of 52 U.S. states and territories it tracked, 48 experienced measles outbreaks during the reporting period between January 2025 and January 2026, PAHO Assistant Director Dr. Rhonda Sealey-Thomas said during the briefing. But Barbosa said the countries are already taking action to control outbreaks, such as strengthening vaccination, surveillance for timely case detection and outbreak response efforts. The U.S. in particular has ramped up vaccination efforts to close immunity gaps, particularly in areas with low immunization coverage experiencing outbreaks, said Dr. Daniel Salas, executive manager of PAHO’s Special Program for Comprehensive Immunization. He said PAHO expects the U.S. to present its corrective action plan in the coming weeks, outlining strategies to regain its designation. In a Tuesday release, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said it will continue to ensure all communities have the resources to prevent and respond to outbreaks and is committed to working with states and other local partners to restore the U.S.’s measles elimination status. CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz said in the release that the measles elimination designation “is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does.” She said the agency has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach communities where transmission is occurring. Last week, Schwartz told Reuters that the agency is going to be “doing the same exact thing that we’ve been doing today” if the U.S. loses its measles-free status. The decision also poses a new test for the Trump administration and its Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who has taken steps to change recommendations for the childhood measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine and has a long history of questioning its safety without evidence. But Kennedy, in a Tuesday X post, said he continues to recommend MMR vaccination as “the most effective way to prevent measles.” “We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust,” Kennedy said. “We will answer families’ questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment.” CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation’s public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases. The resurgence of measles exposes eroding trust in health institutions and growing challenges in persuading people to protect themselves, their children and their communities. Public health experts previously told CNBC that the return of measles ultimately boils down to fewer children getting vaccinated. The MMR vaccine is about 97% effective against measles after two doses, while a single dose is 93% effective, according to the CDC. Yet data aggregated by the CDC shows that vaccination coverage for that shot among U.S. kindergarteners fell to 92.4% in the 2025-2026 school year, which is below the 95% threshold public health officials generally recommend to prevent sustained transmission. The American Medical Association urged the public to get vaccinated after PAHO’s announcement. “We must treat the current measles outbreaks as an urgent call to rebuild trust in science-based recommendations and restore strong, fully-funded public health infrastructure to protect families and communities and prevent the return of diseases once eliminated,” AMA President Dr. Willie Underwood said in a statement. In a separate statement, the pharmaceutical industry’s largest lobbying group called on policymakers to provide “clear, accurate information about the seriousness of measles infection and the effectiveness of vaccines against it, restore and sustain high vaccination coverage in every community, strengthen vaccination efforts, and invest in disease surveillance and rapid outbreak response.” “We have the tools to eliminate measles again and what we need now is the commitment to use them,” said Dr. Michael Ybarra, chief medical officer of the Pharmaceutical Research and Manufacturers of America. Measles isn’t just a public health threat — it’s also a huge financial burden on the healthcare system, CNBC reported. Based on this year’s case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses. The U.S. now joins Canada, Spain and the U.K., which all recently lost their measles-free status after a review by experts at the World Health Organization. While the U.S. dropped out of the WHO, it remains a member of PAHO, its Americas branch. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

美国失去了 麻疹 消除地位,抹去了数十年的进步 - NBC新闻

U.S. loses measles-elimination status, erasing decades of progress - NBC News
新闻简报美国已失去对麻疹的消除状态,这一结论由美洲卫生组织(PAHO)的独立委员会确认,该委员会判定该病毒已连续传播超过一年。这一决定推翻了2000年的一项重大公共卫生成就。截至2026年,已报告超过5,400例麻疹病例,比上一年的病例数增加了一倍多,其中有10例死亡,包括至少4例在尚未接种疫苗的婴儿中。该病毒具有高度传染性,可传播给9/10的密切接触者,并在空气中持续存在长达两小时。美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)及其合作伙伴承诺鼓励MMR 疫苗接种,扩大疫苗接种的可及性,并加强数据共享,但今年他们尚未开展媒体简报或疫苗接种宣传活动。由于近期有政治言论质疑疫苗的安全性,公众对该疫苗的信任已受到动摇,引发了对应对暴发效果的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
泛美卫生组织 (PAHO) 的一个独立委员会在评估 2025 年 1 月至 2026 年 1 月的数据后做出的决定,于 2026 年 11 月 17 日撤销了 麻疹 的消除状态。该文章于世界标准时间 2026 年 11 月 17 日上午 12:41 发表,并于同日晚上 10:12 更新。
地点与对象
该事件发生在美国,特别是在 麻疹 疫苗接种 比率已降至预防 暴发 所需的 95% 阈值以下的社区和县。受影响的人群包括未接种疫苗的人,特别是婴儿和免疫功能低下的人,他们发生严重并发症的风险较高。
事件
经泛美卫生组织 (PAHO) 独立委员会确认,已失去 麻疹 消除状态。该决定是基于该病毒不间断传播了一年多,美国现已被列为流行 麻疹。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年,已记录超过5400例麻疹病例,比上一年的病例数翻了一番多,是自1991年以来的最高水平。今年已有10人因麻疹死亡,其中至少有4例发生在尚未接种疫苗的婴儿身上。该病毒具有高度传染性,可感染近距离接触的9/10人,并可在空气中停留长达两小时。风险尤其高的是未接种疫苗的人群,包括婴儿和免疫系统较弱的人。
应对、状态与不确定性
泛美卫生组织(PAHO)撤销了美国的麻疹-消除地位,预计美国将在未来几周内提交纠正行动计划。CDC 及其合作伙伴表示,他们将鼓励 MMR 疫苗接种、扩大注射机会、加强数据共享并对可衡量的进展负责。然而,CDC 今年尚未通过媒体吹风会或 疫苗接种 活动来回应 暴发,并且由于最近质疑其安全性的政治声明,人们担心公众对 疫苗 的信心。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

U.S. loses measles-elimination status, erasing decades of progress - NBC News

News Brief
The U.S. has lost its measles-elimination status, as confirmed by an independent commission of the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), which determined that the virus has circulated nonstop for over a year. This decision reverses a major public health achievement from 2000. As of 2026, there have been over 5,400 measles cases, more than double the previous year’s count, with ten deaths reported, including at least four in infants too young to be vaccinated. The virus is highly contagious, spreading to 9 out of 10 people in close proximity and lingering in the air for up to two hours. The CDC and its partners have pledged to encourage MMR vaccination, expand access to shots, and strengthen data sharing, but they have not launched media briefings or vaccination campaigns this year. Public confidence in vaccines has been shaken by recent political statements questioning their safety, raising concerns about the effectiveness of the response to the outbreak.
Original source text (English)
0 seconds of 2 minutes, 3 seconds Volume 0% Press shift question mark to access a list of keyboard shortcuts Keyboard Shortcuts Play/Pause SPACE Increase Volume ↑ Decrease Volume ↓ Seek Forward → Seek Backward ← Captions On/Off c Fullscreen/Exit Fullscreen f Mute/Unmute m Decrease Caption Size - Increase Caption Size + or = Seek % 0-9 Live 00:00 02:03 02:03 Measles resurgence costs U.S. long-held elimination status 02:03 Get more news on Nov. 17, 2026, 12:41 AM UTC / Updated Nov. 17, 2026, 10:12 PM UTC By Aria Bendix Measles is no longer eliminated in the United States, reversing one of the country’s greatest public health achievements this century. An independent commission of the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), a group within the World Health Organization, revoked the U.S.’ measles-elimination status after determining that the virus has circulated nonstop for more than a year. PAHO announced the decision Tuesday. “Losing a measles elimination status has broader implications than losing a public health designation,” PAHO’s director, Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, said in a media briefing. “It means the virus has circulated for a prolonged period of time, putting families and communities at risk of a preventable illness, hospitalization and death.” The U.S. earned the elimination status in 2000. Now, measles is once again endemic. “As a pediatrician and a parent, I am heartbroken that we have reached this point,” said Dr. Richard Besser, president of the Robert Wood Johnson Foundation, a health-focused philanthropic organization. “Too many families in America are once again having to worry that their children could get this preventable infection and suffer its most severe consequences.” Upward of 5,400 cases have been recorded this year — more than double last year’s count and the highest since 1991. Ten people have died , according to state health departments. PAHO determines whether countries in the Americas meet the requirements for measles elimination. The group deliberated for four days last week in Washington, D.C., to assess whether transmission in the U.S. and other countries in the region had been sustained from January 2025 to January 2026. In addition to the U.S., the group determined that measles is no longer eliminated in Mexico or Bolivia. A commission made up of eight public health experts evaluated genetic data from “homegrown” cases (those not related to international travel) to figure out whether outbreaks across U.S. states were linked. The commission also weighed levels of vaccination coverage, the quality of measles surveillance and whether there are sufficient strategies and resources to respond to outbreaks. As in many countries, vaccination rates in the U.S. have declined in recent years: 92% of kindergartners had been vaccinated for measles as of the 2025-26 school year, down from 95% — the threshold needed to prevent outbreaks — in the 2019-20 school year. Nationwide among kindergartners, 75% of counties and jurisdictions with measles vaccine data fall below the 95% rate, according to an NBC News investigation . “The United States has an extraordinary measles problem. We’ve had more measles cases in the last 18 months than we’ve had in the previous 25 years combined,” said Noel Brewer, a public health professor at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill. Endemic measles poses a serious risk to people who are unvaccinated, said Dr. Walter Orenstein, former director of the U.S. immunization program at the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. The risk is especially pronounced for infants, who are not typically eligible for the first dose of the vaccine until they turn 1, as well as those who cannot get the shot because they are immunocompromised. “People can go into a supermarket and get infected,” he said. At least four of the measles deaths this year were in babies too young to be vaccinated. Some health departments in outbreak zones are recommending an early dose for infants as young as 6 months. Measles is among the most contagious viruses known to humans: One infected person can spread the virus to 9 out of 10 others in close proximity. It can linger in the air for up to two hours. Infections in unvaccinated people can lead to deadly complications such as pneumonia or brain swelling. Brewer said the U.S. desperately needs a strategy for tackling measles. “The lack of action to address the growing outbreak is clear negligence,” he said. Under past presidential administrations, the CDC has responded to measles outbreaks with media briefings and vaccination campaigns, but the agency has not done so this year. Pan American Health Organization headquarters in Washington, D.C. Tony Quinn / Sipa USA via AP Dr. Daniel Salas, executive manager of PAHO’s Special Program for Comprehensive Immunization, said Tuesday that the U.S. is expected to submit a corrective plan of action in the coming weeks. He added that the country is already taking steps to close immunity gaps in undervaccinated communities. The CDC and its healthcare partners said Tuesday in a joint statement that they will encourage MMR vaccination, expand access to shots, strengthen data sharing and “hold ourselves accountable for measurable progress.” A spokesperson for the Department of Health and Human Services said that the CDC has “mounted an aggressive response to measles outbreaks,” including allocating $100 million in additional funding in October and deploying experts to support state responses. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz “continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles,” the spokesperson said. “We know where transmission is occurring and we’re working with state and local health departments to reach those communities,” Schwartz said in a separate statement. “Losing a designation doesn’t mean we abandon an evidence-based approach. It means we remain focused on stopping outbreaks.” Kennedy said on X that HHS will work to regain the country’s measles elimination status. “We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust. We will answer families’ questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment,” he said. Since taking office, Kennedy has questioned the safety and effectiveness of measles vaccines and touted unproven remedies like inhaled steroids . President Donald Trump, meanwhile, falsely suggested in August that the combined measles shot could be “quite lethal,” despite decades of evidence demonstrating its safety. “We have seen frequent and damaging public comments that have been shaking the confidence of parents about vaccination,” Brewer said. A measles, mumps and rubella vaccine is displayed at a Pennsylvania Department of Health measles vaccination clinic in Lancaster County on Oct. 20. Matt Rourke / AP The pandemic accelerated the downward vaccination trend. Some parents avoided taking their children to the pediatrician , which meant delaying routine shots. An onslaught of misinformation about the Covid vaccines also reignited debunked theories, including the false notion that measles shots are linked to autism. Since 2019, 82% of counties and jurisdictions across the U.S. have reported declines in measles vaccination rates. Three small, rural counties — Catron County, New Mexico, and Adams and Custer counties in Idaho — have rates for kindergartners below 50%. Even before the pandemic, the World Health Organization warned that vaccine hesitancy was contributing to a global resurgence of measles. “It’s not just a U.S. problem, and it’s not just this administration,” Brewer said. Last year, Canada’s measles elimination status was revoked following an outbreak that led to more than 5,000 cases. “It wasn’t for a lack of effort in Canada. There was a political commitment at the highest level to sustain measles elimination and to stop that outbreak,” said Dr. Jon Andrus, a global health professor at George Washington University and the chair of the PAHO commission that determines measles elimination status. “That was for me the sad part of the story, because they really did try.” Barbosa, PAHO’s director, said Tuesday that Canada has interrupted measles transmission in its outbreak but that the country needs to keep up with vaccinations and surveillance to reclaim its elimination status. It could take years, experts said, for the U.S. to do the same. Follow relevant topics Health See your feed ›

Time
measles-elimination status was revoked on November 17, 2026, with the decision made by an independent commission of the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) after assessing data from January 2025 to January 2026. The article was published on November 17, 2026, at 12:41 AM UTC and updated on the same date at 10:12 PM UTC.
Location and population
The event occurred in the United States, specifically in communities and counties where measles vaccination rates have fallen below the 95% threshold needed to prevent outbreaks. The affected population includes unvaccinated individuals, particularly infants and immunocompromised people, who are at higher risk of severe complications.
Event
has lost its measles-elimination status, as confirmed by an independent commission of the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO). The decision was based on the virus circulating nonstop for more than a year, with the U.S. now classified as having endemic measles.
Scale, impact and risk
As of 2026, there have been upward of 5,400 measles cases recorded, more than double the previous year’s count and the highest since 1991. Ten people have died from measles this year, with at least four of these deaths occurring in infants too young to be vaccinated. The virus is highly contagious, spreading to 9 out of 10 people in close proximity, and can linger in the air for up to two hours. The risk is especially high for unvaccinated individuals, including infants and those with weakened immune systems.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) revoked the U.S. measles-elimination status, and the U.S. is expected to submit a corrective plan of action in the coming weeks. The CDC and its partners have stated they will encourage MMR vaccination, expand access to shots, strengthen data sharing, and hold themselves accountable for measurable progress. However, the CDC has not responded to outbreaks with media briefings or vaccination campaigns this year, and there are concerns about public confidence in vaccines due to recent political statements questioning their safety.

美国的麻疹淘汰状态正式消失。谁应该获得加强剂?

The United States’ measles elimination status is officially gone. Who should get a booster?
新闻简报The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status, as declared by the Pan American Health Organization on November 17, 2026. This marks a shift to endemic status due to over a year of continuous local transmission. The CDC reported at least 5,412 confirmed measles cases in the US this year, mostly among unvaccinated individuals. The virus is highly contagious, lingering in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves. The US is expected to submit a corrective action plan to PAHO, and the CDC has allocated $100 million to address the issue. Efforts include closing vaccination gaps, rebuilding public trust, and providing guidance for diagnosing and treating measles. The measles vaccine, MMR, is recommended for children and adults, with two doses offering 97% protection. The situation requires urgent action to prevent further spread and protect communities.

地点与对象
美国,重点关注经历 暴发 的社区。Jarbas Barbosa da Silva Jr. 博士表示,麻疹病毒 也已在玻利维亚和墨西哥重新建立。

美国已正式失去 麻疹 消除地位,麻疹 现在被认为是该国的流行病。这是由于本地传播持续一年多,今年美国报告了超过5,412例麻疹确诊病例,其中大部分是未接种疫苗的人群。
规模、影响与风险
今年美国至少报告了 5,412 例 麻疹 确诊病例,接触几率远高于几十年来。麻疹病毒具有高度传染性,即使感染者离开后,它也可以在空气中停留至少两个小时。CDC表示,麻疹是世界上传染性最强的病毒之一,目前的情况需要采取紧急行动。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

The United States’ measles elimination status is officially gone. Who should get a booster?

News Brief
The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status, as declared by the Pan American Health Organization on November 17, 2026. This marks a shift to endemic status due to over a year of continuous local transmission. The CDC reported at least 5,412 confirmed measles cases in the US this year, mostly among unvaccinated individuals. The virus is highly contagious, lingering in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves. The US is expected to submit a corrective action plan to PAHO, and the CDC has allocated $100 million to address the issue. Efforts include closing vaccination gaps, rebuilding public trust, and providing guidance for diagnosing and treating measles. The measles vaccine, MMR, is recommended for children and adults, with two doses offering 97% protection. The situation requires urgent action to prevent further spread and protect communities.
Original source text (English)
Although measles boosters aren’t typically recommended, certain people could use another shot, especially with so many outbreaks ongoing in the US. Matt Rourke/AP See all topics The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status, the Pan American Health Organization announced Tuesday. Measles is once again considered endemic in the country, due to more than a year of continuous local transmission. Measles is also re-established in Bolivia and Mexico, Dr. Jarbas Barbosa da Silva Jr., director of PAHO, said Tuesday, adding that the current situation requires “urgent action.” “Losing a measles elimination status has broader implications than losing a public health designation. It means the virus has circulated for a prolonged period of time, putting families and communities at risk of preventable illness, hospitalization, and death,” Barbosa said. Dr. Peter Hotez, co-director of the Center for Vaccine Development at Texas Children’s Hospital in Houston, put it simply: “Basically, measles is back.” Countries that lose elimination status are required to submit a corrective action plan to PAHO that outlines how they’ll regain elimination status. The US is expected to submit its plan in the coming weeks, Barbosa said. “We will stop measles outbreaks, protect American families, and work to regain U.S. measles elimination status. I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles,” US Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. said in a social media post Tuesday. “We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust. We will answer families’ questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment. We will support Americans who become ill, regardless of vaccination status, and for the first time equip health care providers with guidance to diagnose and treat measles and its complications.” Dr. Erica Schwartz, director of the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, said Tuesday that the agency knows where transmission is occurring and that it has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities. “The measles elimination designation is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does,” Schwartz said in a statement. “Losing a designation doesn’t mean we abandon an evidence-based approach. It means we remain focused on stopping outbreaks.” The United States held its measles elimination status for 26 years, and the vast majority of people in the US are protected from the highly contagious virus. But doctors say that if you haven’t been vaccinated, now is the time. Although measles boosters aren’t typically recommended, certain people could use another shot, especially with so many outbreaks ongoing in the US. The country has tallied more than 5,000 cases this year, and the chance of being exposed to the virus is much higher than it has been in decades. What the numbers say At least 5,412 confirmed measles cases have been reported in the United States this year, mostly among unvaccinated people, according to the CDC . Experts say that figure is most likely an undercount. This is the highest number of infections in the US in more than three decades, and unless many more people get vaccinated, it will probably continue to grow. Compared with the millions of people who get the flu each year, 5,000-plus cases may not sound like a lot. But measles is one of the most contagious viruses in the world. If a person with measles walks into a room with 10 unvaccinated people, about nine would catch the virus, research suggests. By comparison, the flu would probably spread to only one person. And measles sticks around: Even after an infected person leaves the room, the virus can linger in the air for at least two hours. A few years ago, when the US had only a handful of cases, most of them happened when someone traveled to countries where the disease was endemic and returned with the virus. Now, most cases in the US result from exposure to someone in the neighborhood, research shows , so it may be at your door sooner than you think. Most people in the US have been vaccinated against the measles, but cases are rising because the vaccination rate has fallen. Experts say that about 95% of a population must be vaccinated to reach what’s called herd or community immunity: when there are enough people vaccinated that the virus can’t get a foothold and will fade away. Before the vaccine was introduced in the US in 1963, the country had 3 million to 4 million measles cases every year, and 400 to 500 people died from the disease annually. Once communities reached the 95% threshold, case numbers fell to almost nothing. What to know about the vaccine The measles vaccine was combined with mumps and rubella vaccines in 1971 to help streamline childhood immunization schedules, improve vaccination rates and reduce the number of shots children would need . The vaccine can be given to older children and adults, as well. And if an unvaccinated person is exposed to the virus, getting a shot within 72 hours could offer some protection or at least result in less serious illness, the CDC says. The MMR vaccine is considered extremely safe. There may be mild side effects, such as soreness, redness and a mild rash at the injection site. Some children may briefly develop a fever or joint pain. Rare risks include febrile seizures, which happen in about 1 in 3,000 to 4,000 children, resolve on their own and cause no long-term harm. Serious allergic reactions are extremely rare. Although a later-retracted study in the 1990s suggested a link between the MMR vaccine and autism, large-scale research involving millions of children has established that there is no link. Who gets the MMR shot Typically, the American Academy of Pediatrics recommends that children get the first dose of the combined MMR vaccine between 12 and 15 months old and a second dose between ages 4 and 6. But anyone older than that can also get vaccinated. With a flu or Covid-19 vaccine, doses are recommended every year because those viruses mutate more quickly. But the measles virus stays pretty much the same from year to year, and two doses of the MMR vaccine should provide good protection for life. For people who live in a region where there’s an outbreak, some doctors suggest giving a dose of the MMR vaccine between 6 and 11 months of age in what’s called a zero dose. It’s then recommended that the child receive the first and second doses on the usual schedule. Immunologist Dr. Elisabeth Adkins Marnik says you can also ask your pediatrician to give your child the second dose of the vaccine before age 4. “I personally made the decision to get my daughter a second dose early when measles started circulating so much in the community and I knew we were also going to be doing a lot of traveling,” said Marnik, a science communicator who works to translate evidence into clear and empathetic public communication. “I just didn’t want to take any risks, and getting it a little bit early is fine. It just gave us extra peace of mind.” Who might need more protection There are others groups whose protection might be lacking and who can be vulnerable to infection, or who might be uncertain about whether they’re protected. Here’s what you need to know: If you were born before 1957 The CDC considers adults born before 1957 to be immune to measles, essentially because, before the vaccine, nearly every child caught the virus at some point. If you’ve gotten one dose A single dose of the measles-mumps-rubella vaccine is considered 93% effective at preventing measles. In fact, only one dose was recommended until 1989, when the US government changed the recommendation to two doses after a measles outbreak among vaccinated school-age children. Two doses of MMR boost measles protection to 97%. If you were vaccinated before 1989 and did not receive a second shot, you may want to consider getting one now. If you’ve gotten two doses Doctors don’t typically suggest boosters for people who received two doses of MMR. If you aren’t sure If you don’t know whether you were vaccinated as a child or whether you have been fully vaccinated, talk to your healthcare provider, who may want to give you another shot. Other people who might want an additional shot are those who received the first version of the measles vaccine between 1963 and 1968, which used a killed virus and may not offer as much protection. Testing for measles immunity You can get tested at a doctor’s office or pharmacy. When you have an infection or get vaccinated, your immune cells produce antibodies against the virus that can stay in your body for years and help you fight infection. A simple blood test looks for these antibodies to determine whether you have immunity. Doctors also sometimes order these tests for people with weakened immune systems to see how much immunity they have. The CDC does not recommend this kind of testing, and doctors say it is not necessary if you have had two doses of the vaccine. But this titer test may give you some peace of mind. “Titer tests are not perfect, but certainly if they’re positive, a person likely has a good bit of protection,” said Dr. Jesse Goodman, a professor of medicine and infectious disease at Georgetown University and director of its Center on Medical Product Access, Safety and Stewardship. “But if you’re pretty sure you didn’t get vaccinated or you didn’t get a second dose, you might as well just go ahead. There’s no harm in being vaccinated again.” Measles can cause serious problems The disease can cause serious symptoms. About 11% of Americans who have had measles this year were so sick that they had to go to the hospital, according to the CDC . There have also been at least 10 deaths in people who had measles, according to state data — the most the country has seen in decades. Measles can cause a rash, fever, red eyes, sensitivity to light and cough. It may also lead to severe complications such as pneumonia or encephalitis, a swelling of the brain that can cause convulsions, deafness or brain damage. For pregnant people, measles may cause premature birth or miscarriage , and they can pass the virus to their baby. Measles can also trigger immune amnesia , a condition in which the immune system forgets how to fight other diseases. Some groups are more vulnerable to measles complications, including older adults, infants and young children, as well as people who have suppressed immune systems because they take certain medications or are undergoing cancer treatment. But anyone can develop severe complications. “Most children who get measles as young children are fine and recover, but it can make them pretty sick. Anywhere from 1 in 5 to 1 in 10 even healthy children will end up in the hospital,” Goodman said. Spreading the word Even if you have been vaccinated — and chances are, you have — herd immunity grows as more people get the shot. Although trust in the vaccine has slipped recently, most people still say they believe in it. Studies show that peers and trusted community members can be persuasive, particularly because it can take time to convince people who are reluctant to get the shot. Marnik advises not to get discouraged if people you know don’t change their minds right away. Misinformation about vaccines is increasingly sophisticated, and it can be spread through close friends, anonymous voices online or even leaders . “So it’s no wonder parents are confused and have questions.” Talk to people who are hesitant about vaccines with empathy, she says. Most people just want what is best for their children. “Oftentimes, it takes time and multiple conversations with somebody who is hesitant for them to entertain potentially making the decision to get vaccinated,” Marnik said. “Losing elimination status is a sad thing, but it really makes it so much more urgent to get vaccinated, because now we know that the likelihood people are going to be exposed to measles is so much higher. “I think we can course-correct. We can rebuild trust. It’s just going to take time,” she said. “This didn’t happen overnight, and we’re not going to be able to change it overnight.” CNN’s Deidre McPhillips and Brenda Goodman contributed to this report. The U.S. has lost measles elimination status. Here's what that means and who is most at risk. - CBS News
新闻简报泛美卫生组织证实,由于 麻疹 病例大幅增加和人群免疫力下降,美国已正式失去 麻疹 消除状态。2026 年,CDC 报告了 5,400 多例 麻疹 病例,是 2025 年的两倍多,其中 590 多人住院治疗,至少有 6 例与 麻疹 相关的死亡。暴发 于 2025 年开始,已蔓延到 45 个州和华盛顿特区,其中宾夕法尼亚州、威斯康星州和纽约州报告影响最严重。消除状态的丧失表明持续的 传播 和持续的 暴发 的风险。包括 CDC 主任 Erica Schwartz 和卫生与公众服务部部长 Robert F. Kennedy 在内的卫生官员强调了 MM​​R 疫苗 在预防 麻疹 和恢复消除状态方面的重要性。他们还强调需要解决错误信息并提高 疫苗接种 率,以遏制疾病传播并保护弱势群体,例如年龄太小而无法接种疫苗的幼儿。

时间
全球卫生官员于 2026 年 11 月 16 日星期二宣布,美国已正式失去 麻疹 消除状态。今年迄今已记录超过 5,400 例 麻疹 病例,是去年总数的两倍多。迄今为止,CDC 已统计 2026 年有 5,412 例病例,导致 590 多人住院治疗,至少有 6 例与 麻疹 相关的死亡,一些州报告了更多死亡病例。
地点与对象
美国,特别是 45 个州和华盛顿特区,已确诊 麻疹 病例。暴发于2025年开始并持续蔓延。今年大约三分之一的 麻疹 案件与这些 麻疹 有关。宾夕法尼亚州、威斯康星州和纽约州等一些州报告了重大病例和死亡病例。
事件
经泛美卫生组织确认,已失去 麻疹 消除状态。由于人群免疫力下降,这种状态消失,导致疾病持续传播。CDC 在 2026 年报告了 5,400 多例 麻疹 病例,其中 590 多人住院治疗,至少有 6 例与 麻疹 相关的死亡。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年记录了超过 5,400 起 麻疹 案件,是去年总数的两倍多。这已导致超过 590 人住院治疗和至少 6 人与 麻疹 相关的死亡,一些州报告了更多死亡人数。失去消除地位意味着 传播 存在持续链的风险以及 麻疹 可能出口到其他国家的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
The CDC has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach communities with measles outbreaks. Health officials are focused on stopping outbreaks and increasing vaccination rates. health and human services secretary has stated that the nation will stop measles outbreaks and work to regain elimination status. The CDC and other health officials are emphasizing the importance of the MMR vaccine and addressing misinformation to improve vaccination rates.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

The U.S. has lost measles elimination status. Here's what that means and who is most at risk. - CBS News

News Brief
The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status, as confirmed by the Pan American Health Organization, due to a significant rise in measles cases and a decline in population immunity. In 2026, the CDC reported over 5,400 measles cases, more than double the number from 2025, with more than 590 hospitalizations and at least six measles-related deaths. The outbreaks, which began in 2025, have spread across 45 states and Washington, D.C., with Pennsylvania, Wisconsin, and New York reporting the most severe impacts. The loss of elimination status indicates sustained transmission and the risk of ongoing outbreaks. Health officials, including CDC Director Erica Schwartz and Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, have emphasized the importance of the MMR vaccine in preventing measles and restoring elimination status. They also highlighted the need to address misinformation and improve vaccination rates to curb the spread of the disease and protect vulnerable populations, such as young children who are too young to be vaccinated.

Here's what that means and who is most at risk. By Kerry Breen Kerry Breen News Editor Kerry Breen is a news editor at CBSNews.com. A graduate of New York University's Arthur L. Carter School of Journalism, she previously worked at NBC News' TODAY Digital. She covers current events, breaking news and issues including substance use. Read Full Bio Kerry Breen Updated on: November 17, 2026 / 4:24 PM EST / CBS News Add CBS News on Google The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status, global health officials announced Tuesday. The decision from the Pan American Health Organization came after the U.S. recorded over 5,400 measles cases so far this year — more than double last year's total. The Pan American Health Organization is a regional office for the World Health Organization, covering North and South America. Researchers with knowledge of the decision had previously confirmed it to CBS News partner KFF Health News on Saturday. A country is considered as having eliminated the measles if there are "no locally transmitted measles infections or outbreaks lasting 12 months or longer," according to the World Health Organization. The United States did not meet that standard for the first time since 2000. According to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, about a third of this year's measles cases are linked to outbreaks that began in 2025 and continued to spread. After a 30-year record of 2,242 cases last year, the CDC has tallied 5,412 cases to date in 2026, resulting in more than 590 hospitalizations and at least six measles-related deaths, with some states reporting additional fatalities. "It's profoundly sad and disappointing," Dr. William Schaffner, a professor of preventive medicine and infectious diseases at the Vanderbilt University Medical Center, told CBS News. "What a tragedy. We have had children die of measles this past year. That should never have happened. Those deaths and all those cases, 5,000-plus, were completely preventable." The biggest outbreak has been in Pennsylvania, where state health officials have reported more than 1,500 cases and seven deaths associated with the disease this year. Wisconsin reports two children have died and New York had its first measles death in more than 30 years. Measles cases have been confirmed so far this year in 45 states and Washington, D.C. "The measles elimination designation is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does," CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz said in a statement. "We know where transmission is occurring and we've invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities." She said the CDC remains focused on stopping outbreaks. Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. said Tuesday on social media, "We will stop measles outbreaks, protect American families, and work to regain U.S. measles elimination status. I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles." Kennedy pointed to "COVID-era disruptions" in childhood vaccinations and cited other countries, including Canada, Mexico, Spain and the United Kingdom, that have also lost measles elimination status. Sen. Bill Cassidy, a Republican from Louisiana who is also a doctor, said health officials, including Kennedy, should be doing more to promote the importance of vaccination to protect against measles. "Speaking as a physician, you may decide not to be vaccinated because it's your free will, but the child who's too young to be vaccinated is vulnerable if you come into that child's presence, and we're seeing children die from measles who are too young to be vaccinated," Cassidy said on "Face the Nation with Margaret Brennan." "So if you've not been vaccinated, you could be the person that brings death to another. That's something to remember." What does it mean to lose measles elimination status? A country or region loses its measles elimination status once its population immunity lowers enough that there is sustained spread of the disease, CBS News medical correspondent Dr. Céline Gounder explained. When a country has measles elimination status and a high rate of vaccination, a case of measles or small outbreak will soon fizzle out. But when there are this many gaps in the population's immunity, there can be "ongoing chains of transmission," Gounder said. "It means that we're at risk of being exposed to measles and getting measles if we're not vaccinated," Gounder said. "It means ERs and pediatricians need to consider measles in anyone with a fever and rash, whether they've traveled overseas or not. And we may now become an exporter of measles to other countries." Dr. Omer Awan, a trained epidemiologist and senior public health contributor for Forbes, said that the loss of measles elimination status is a setback to the nation's public health infrastructure. "It's frustrating and heartbreaking ... because we have the tools to fight this. The tool is the MMR vaccine, which we know is 97% effective at preventing the onset of measles," Awan said. "It'd be one thing if we didn't have the tools to fight this, but the tools are at our disposal, we just aren't using the tools that can help us mitigate the spread and beat measles." How can the U.S. regain measles elimination status? A country earns measles elimination status by ensuring there is no local spread of the disease or outbreaks that last for more than a year. PAHO says that the best way to maintain the elimination of measles is maintaining a vaccination rate of 95% or higher. Countries should also have sensitive surveillance systems to detect cases quickly, laboratory capacity and a guaranteed rapid response to outbreaks. Data about these trends must be submitted to and verified by the organization. For the U.S. to curb outbreaks and regain measles elimination status, it needs to return to higher rates of vaccination, Awan said. He said battling misinformation and ramping up vaccination campaigns would be key to that effort. Data shows childhood vaccination rates have declined in many communities around the U.S. in recent years, often below the threshold needed to help ward off measles outbreaks. Gounder also noted that lawmakers can create policies that lead to increased vaccination rates. "States set the school vaccine rules. Nine states made opting out of vaccination easier in 2025. States ending nonmedical exemptions saw kindergarten vaccination rise 2 to 4 points," Gounder said. "Most legislatures reconvene in January. Your state lawmakers will write next year's exemption rules." Measles in the U.S. The United States started collecting national data on measles in 1912, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. About 6,000 measles-related deaths were reported each year in the first decade of that data collection. In the decade between 1953 and 1963, nearly all children in the U.S. caught measles before they turned 15, with an estimated 3 to 4 million people infected each year and tens of thousands hospitalized, according to the CDC. In those years, 400 to 500 people, mostly children, died of measles annually. Then in 1963, the measles vaccine first became widely available. The numbers began a sharp decline and several generations of Americans grew up without ever encountering measles. By 1981, reported measles cases had dropped by 80% compared to the previous year, the CDC said. The country was officially declared as having eliminated measles in 2000. The U.S. maintained its elimination status for over 25 years. That didn't mean there were no cases at all — in 2015, an outbreak that spread among unvaccinated people was traced back to overseas visitors at Disneyland in Southern California, and a 2018-19 outbreak in New York was eventually contained by public health measures including curtailing vaccine exemptions. But those spikes were just a fraction of the spread now being seen in the U.S. The nation recorded 2,242 measles cases in 2025, according to CDC data, linked to 48 separate outbreaks. The CDC reported 245 hospitalizations, mostly in children, and three measles deaths last year. Infections were confirmed in at least 44 states. It was the most cases the country had seen in over three decades. That record was shattered in 2026, when the U.S. recorded more than double that number of cases by October — a total that topped 5,000 earlier this month. Almost all of the cases have been in people who were not vaccinated. Measles-related deaths have been reported in Pennsylvania, New York and Wisconsin. The CDC said 43 new outbreaks — defined as three or more related cases — have been reported so far in 2026, while about a third of the cases reported this year are tied to outbreaks that have been ongoing since 2025. Who is at risk from measles? Measles is one of the most contagious infectious diseases. Those who are unvaccinated for the disease are most at risk of getting it. CDC data shows that 96% of people infected with measles in 2026 had not been vaccinated for measles or were unsure of their vaccination status; only 2% of cases were in people who'd been fully vaccinated. Medical research has shown that two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine is extremely safe and effective at protecting against measles. But some people — including babies, pregnant women or people with weakened immune systems — cannot get vaccinated and must rely on population immunity to keep the disease from spreading. "They will be the ones that are most vulnerable to getting infected and experiencing the complications," such as pneumonia or encephalitis, Awan said. Many of the recent cases have been occurring in tight-knit and largely unvaccinated Amish or Mennonite communities. "It really shows you who's vulnerable because these are totally unvaccinated communities," said Dr. Deborah Birx, a longtime public health official who was a leader of the White House Coronavirus Task Force during the pandemic. "You can see it in who's hospitalized. The largest percentage are children under 4 and adults over 50." "I hope this is a wake-up call to every parent," Birx told CBS News. "If you had decided to not vaccinate your children, you've been relatively protected by all of us and all of our grandchildren and other children who got vaccinated. But that protection and that shield is going away" as vaccination rates slip. "And so your child is now vulnerable at a level they never were vulnerable before." Measles is known for causing a rash, but Schaffner said that's the least of it. "What's bothersome is children, even with uncomplicated measles, are miserable for a week or a week and a half with red eyes, runny nose, cough, and just debilitating fever and diarrhea. And then you have the complications of dehydration, pneumonia, and on occasion, encephalitis." There can be other lingering health consequences, Birx warned. Measles patients "can end up with what we call immune suppression, so that they're more susceptible to all other diseases after they've had measles." While many people recover, measles can cause severe infections in the lungs and brain that can lead to cognitive issues, deafness or death. "Measles is a nasty, nasty virus," Schaffner said. "In contrast, the vaccine is wonderfully effective." 地点与对象
具体来说,从持续性中枢神经系统病例中分离出的 基因组 始终编码非功能性基质 (M) 蛋白以及超融合融合 (F) 糖蛋白。
事件
此外,使用带有截短胞质尾的麻疹病毒糖蛋白假型化的水泡性口炎病毒颗粒的进入测定证实了表达CD150的细胞中进入能力的降低,证实了与典型受体依赖性进入的功能转变。

潜伏期的相似性(例如在斑海豹中观察到的 12 至 13 年的差距)为将这些动物麻疹病毒与人类 麻疹病毒 进行比较提供了基线。

目前尚不清楚这些突变谱是否代表了播种 突变(对于病毒最初穿过血脑屏障至关重要),或者它们是否是长期 进化 和病毒在中枢神经系统中建立立足点后发生的适应的结果。
