Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, November 21, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Animal Morbilliviruses Mirror Genetic Mutations Driving Fatal Human Measles Brain Complications by November 17, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 7 mins read 0 Animal Morbilliviruses Mirror Genetic Mutations Driving Fatal Human Measles Brain Complications Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Persistent central nervous system infections caused by animal morbilliviruses share fundamental genetic and phenotypic characteristics with human measles virus variants that cause subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, according to a comparative analysis published in One Health Outlook by Rik L. de Swart and Rory D. de Vries examining recent findings from Daniel Roji and colleagues. Morbilliviruses persisting in wild animal hosts evolve analogous molecular mechanisms that favor long-term viral maintenance in brain tissue, characterized primarily by the inactivation of the matrix protein and the acquisition of hyperfusogenic fusion proteins. Measles virus is an enveloped pathogen possessing a negative-strand RNA genome and represents one of the most infectious viral agents affecting humans. Transmitted via the respiratory route, measles virus initiates infection by targeting myeloid and lymphoid cells through the high-affinity cellular receptor CD150, also known as SLAMF1. This initial phase generates a cell-associated viremia that disseminates the virus throughout host lymphoid tissues, accompanied by infected immune cell infiltration into peripheral organs. In later stages of pathogenesis, the virus utilizes a second receptor, nectin-4, located at epithelial adherens junctions to infect epithelial cells and keratinocytes. The widespread depletion of memory immune cells induces lymphopenia and prolonged immune amnesia, leaving patients susceptible to opportunistic secondary infections such as pneumonia, diarrhoea, and otitis media. While the adaptive immune response typically clears acute measles and provides lifelong immunity, measles virus can in rare instances establish a persistent central nervous system infection. This persistent state can resurface after a clinically silent incubation period lasting several years as subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, a neurological disease that is invariably fatal. Therapeutic interventions currently available provide at best transient clinical improvement, and the infection relentlessly progresses despite high concentrations of measles-specific antibodies in both serum and cerebrospinal fluid. Genomic characterization of measles virus recovered from the brains of deceased subacute sclerosing panencephalitis patients reveals extensive biased hypermutation, often displaying profiles characteristic of adenosine deaminase acting on RNA (ADAR1) activity. Although specific mutational patterns diverge across individual clinical cases, they exhibit marked functional convergence. Specifically, genomes isolated from persistent central nervous system cases consistently encode nonfunctional matrix (M) proteins alongside hyperfusogenic fusion (F) glycoproteins. Inactivation of the matrix gene occurs through premature stop codons, corruption of the translation initiation codon, or hypermutation yielding inactive proteins. Because virion budding requires structural interaction between the matrix protein and the cytoplasmic tail of the fusion glycoprotein, loss of matrix function abolishes the assembly and release of infectious viral particles. Concurrently, mutations in the fusion gene destabilize the fusion trimer, driving a hyperfusogenic phenotype that permits cell-to-cell spread even in host cells lacking CD150 or nectin-4 receptors. In neural tissues where standard morbillivirus receptors are absent, the synaptic cell adhesion molecules CADM1 and CADM2 can trigger viral membrane fusion in cis. This receptor-independent fusion mechanism facilitates the gradual, progressive transfer of viral ribonucleoprotein complexes across synaptic junctions, resulting in widespread brain infection in the absence of budding virions. Related animal morbilliviruses demonstrate comparable pathogenic trajectories across their natural host species. Canine distemper virus in carnivores, phocine distemper virus in seals, and cetacean morbillivirus in whales and dolphins frequently trigger severe disease outbreaks. While canine distemper virus often produces acute neurological complications reminiscent of measles inclusion body encephalitis due to severe immune suppression, it can also produce delayed encephalitis. For instance, old dog encephalitis develops years after initial canine distemper infection, marked by chronic demyelination and persistent viral antigen despite the inability to isolate infectious virus. Similar delayed, chronic central nervous system infections have been observed in stranded harbor seals and dolphins. In the primary study evaluated by de Swart and de Vries, Daniel Roji and colleagues characterized the genomes of morbilliviruses that persisted for multiple years in wild animal brains: canine distemper virus in a lynx, cetacean morbillivirus in a striped dolphin, and phocine distemper virus in two harbor seals. Notably, the harbor seals stranded in 2001 and 2014, approximately 13 and 12 years after major phocine distemper epizootics in 1988 and 2001, mirroring the prolonged latency period observed in human subacute sclerosing panencephalitis. Genomic analysis showed that the viral sequences from the lynx, the dolphin, and one of the seals harbored premature stop codons or hypermutation disrupting the matrix protein, together with mutations conferring thermal instability and hyperfusogenicity in the fusion protein. To evaluate the biological effects of these mutations, the researchers conducted in vitro fusion assays in Vero cells with and without expression of species-specific CD150 receptors from seals, dolphins, dogs, and humans. In three of the four evaluated viruses, cell-to-cell fusion in receptor-negative cells was markedly increased. Furthermore, entry assays using vesicular stomatitis virus particles pseudotyped with morbillivirus glycoproteins bearing truncated cytoplasmic tails confirmed reduced entry capacity in CD150-expressing cells, corroborating the functional shift away from canonical receptor-dependent entry. Detailed experimental investigation of the lynx canine distemper virus isolate revealed that a single amino acid substitution in the fusion protein, designated P479A, was sufficient to explain the hyperfusogenic phenotype. Recombinant canine distemper virus harboring this P479A substitution displayed severe attenuation in CD150-expressing Vero cells and required passaging exclusively through infected canine B cells. However, despite impaired propagation in lymphoid cells, the mutant virus exhibited enhanced infectivity and dissemination in organotypic brain slice cultures derived from ferrets. This shift closely parallels previous experimental findings by Schmitz and colleagues, where chimeric measles virus bearing a fusion gene from a human subacute sclerosing panencephalitis case lost lymphotropism while efficiently propagating through human neural networks. The study highlights how naturally occurring animal morbillivirus infections provide critical insights into human neuropathology, particularly given the absence of tractable animal models that reproduce the multi-year latency of subacute sclerosing panencephalitis. Emerging organoid cultures allow researchers to evaluate the functional consequences of specific viral mutations, yet they cannot determine whether such mutations act as initial seeding requirements for neuroinvasion or emerge during subsequent intra-host persistence. A comparative One Health framework tracking natural morbillivirus infections in diverse hosts could identify predictive molecular biomarkers, potentially enabling the early detection of persistent central nervous system infection before fatal neurological damage occurs. Expanding the current understanding of viral persistence, Roji et al. argue that naturally occurring animal infections offer a distinct advantage for studying subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE). Because clinical reports of SSPE typically focus on the terminal stage of disease, baseline knowledge of the progression during years of silent incubation is limited. By studying animal cases across various species, the researchers suggest that these instances function as natural experiments. This One Health perspective identifies molecular signatures that might not be observable in controlled, short-term laboratory studies. The similarity in latency—such as the 12 to 13-year gaps observed in harbor seals—provides a baseline for comparing these animal morbilliviruses to the human measles virus. Such comparisons offer a platform to investigate the host-specific drivers that allow for prolonged persistence within the central nervous system. To establish these functional parallels, the study team utilized a tiered experimental approach to link specific genomic mutations to viral behavior. Initial fusion assays conducted in Vero cells provided a controlled environment to observe how the presence or absence of host-specific CD150 receptors influenced viral activity. The researchers tested the interaction of CD150 from multiple species, including seal, dolphin, dog, and human origins. A critical finding was the observation that most of these persistent virus variants exhibited enhanced membrane fusion in cells that lacked the high-affinity receptors typically required by morbilliviruses. To further test this, the authors used vesicular stomatitis virus particles pseudotyped with morbillivirus glycoproteins. These experiments specifically focused on particles with truncated cytoplasmic tails. By demonstrating reduced entry in cells expressing CD150, the team confirmed a shift in functional requirements, moving the viruses away from canonical receptor-dependent entry and favoring receptor-independent cell-to-cell spread. The study provided an in-depth analysis of the CDV isolate derived from a lynx, identifying a specific mutation in the fusion gene, P479A, as the primary determinant for the hyperfusogenic phenotype. While the team was unable to isolate the live virus directly from the lynx brain tissue, the generation of recombinant CDV strains allowed for detailed testing. These engineered viruses confirmed that the presence of the P479A amino acid change resulted in clear attenuation within standard Vero-CD150 cultures. When forced to propagate, these mutant viruses could only thrive in canine B-cells. However, when these recombinant strains were introduced to organotypic brain slice cultures derived from ferrets, the virus showed efficient spread. This specific observation strongly mirrors experimental results from previous studies on SSPE-associated measles virus, where researchers demonstrated that chimeric viruses containing the fusion gene from an SSPE patient lost their ability to infect lymphoid cells but gained the capacity for efficient dissemination throughout neural networks. Despite these advancements, the authors highlight significant limitations. It remains unclear whether these mutational profiles represent seeding mutations—essential for the virus to initially cross the blood-brain barrier—or if they are the consequence of long-term evolution and adaptation occurring after the virus has already established a foothold in the central nervous system. Without longitudinal models that capture the entire span of the infection, this distinction remains a critical gap. The researchers note that while newer organoid-based models provide higher resolution for examining functional changes, they currently offer only a snapshot rather than a predictive timeline. Looking forward, the study aims to refine the use of comparative pathogenesis as a diagnostic tool. The ultimate goal for this One Health approach is to identify molecular signatures that hold predictive value for disease progression. If researchers can establish a link between genomic traits and the likelihood of chronic persistence, it could lead to the development of early biomarkers. Detecting these patterns during the clinically silent phase could eventually enable clinicians to initiate targeted antiviral interventions before the virus causes irreparable damage. By mapping the evolutionary pathways that consistently lead to CNS persistence across diverse hosts, the researchers believe it is possible to transition to a more proactive strategy, improving the management of this condition. Location and population
Specifically, genomes isolated from persistent central nervous system cases consistently encode nonfunctional matrix (M) proteins alongside hyperfusogenic fusion (F) glycoproteins.
Event
Furthermore, entry assays using vesicular stomatitis virus particles pseudotyped with morbillivirus glycoproteins bearing truncated cytoplasmic tails confirmed reduced entry capacity in CD150-expressing cells, corroborating the functional shift away from canonical receptor-dependent entry.
Scale, impact and risk
The similarity in latency—such as the 12 to 13-year gaps observed in harbor seals—provides a baseline for comparing these animal morbilliviruses to the human measles virus.
Response, status and uncertainty

经过多年的 麻疹 衰退,美国正式失去 麻疹 淘汰地位

United States officially loses measles elimination status after years of vaccination decline
新闻简报由于病毒传播一年多,美国自 2026 年 11 月 13 日起正式失去 麻疹 消除状态。2025 年,45 个州确诊了 2,289 例 麻疹 病例,造成 3 人死亡。2026年,47个州的病例数已增至5,412例,其中6人死亡。美国政府正在努力识别高风险社区并改进 疫苗接种 工作,但现任政府不支持 CDC 的儿童 疫苗接种 建议。
查看新闻五要素
时间
13日,由于该病毒已传播一年多。
地点与对象
这一决定是泛美卫生组织在26年前获得这一地位后做出的。
事件
由于病毒传播一年多,美国已正式失去 麻疹 消除状态。
规模、影响与风险
2025 年,45 个州确诊了 2,289 例 麻疹 病例,造成 3 人死亡。2026年,47个州已有5,412例确诊病例,其中6例确诊死亡。由于病毒的持续传播,风险级别很高。
应对、状态与不确定性
将为州、地方、部落和地区卫生部门提供工具和技术支持,以识别风险较高的社区,与值得信赖的合作伙伴合作,快速发现病例,并有效应对 暴发。当前的行政部门不支持 CDC 的子 疫苗接种 建议。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

United States officially loses measles elimination status after years of vaccination decline

News Brief
The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status as of November 13, 2026, due to the virus circulating for over a year. In 2025, there were 2,289 confirmed measles cases across 45 states, causing three deaths. In 2026, the number of cases has risen to 5,412 in 47 states, with six deaths. The U.S. government is working to identify high-risk communities and improve vaccination efforts, but the current administration does not support CDC’s child vaccination recommendations.
Original source text (English)
Skip to Content Open Navigation Menu The Observer About Staff Contact Us Letters to the editor Farewells Application The Observer Open Search Bar Search this site Submit Search Open Navigation Menu The Observer About The Observer Contact News Life Opinion Sports Fun Videos The Observer Open Search Bar Search this site Submit Search Open Navigation Menu The Observer About The Observer Contact News Life Opinion Sports Fun Videos The Observer Open Search Bar Search this site Submit Search Categories: News United States officially loses measles elimination status after years of vaccination decline Hana Ruran, Contributing Writer November 20, 2026 The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status as of Nov. 13, due to the fact that the virus has circulated for over a year. This decision was made by the Pan American Health Organization after the status was granted 26 years ago. The recent spread could be attributed to the decline in immunization rates of the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine. Nara and Dvids On Nov. 13, the United States lost its measles elimination status. The decision was made by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) during an annual review meeting held from Nov. 10 through Nov. 13, in which they concluded that the measles virus had circulated continuously within the country for longer than 12 months. This ended the U.S.’s 26-year-long measles elimination status record, which was initially declared in 2000. For measles to be declared “eliminated” in a country, it must have an “absence of endemic measles transmission in a defined geographical area for [at least] 12 months in the presence of a well-performing surveillance system,” according to the World Health Organization (WHO). In the case of the U.S., the virus has been reported to be spreading locally from person to person. Earlier this year, the U.S. National Sustainability Committee for the Elimination of Measles, Rubella, and Congenital Rubella Syndrome and committee chair, Noel T. Brewer, responsible for reviewing the CDC’s evidence and informing the PAHO’s decision regarding the U.S. ‘s measles elimination status, warned that the U.S. could lose its status. “Regardless of the outcome of our meeting and PAHO’s decision in November, the U.S. clearly has a measles problem,” Brewer said. Reasons for the prior speculation of the U.S. losing its measles elimination status include the fact that in 2025, the country faced 2,289 confirmed cases of measles across 45 states, causing three deaths. As of Nov. 12 of this year, there have been 5,412 confirmed measles cases in the U.S. among 47 states, including six confirmed deaths. In 2000, there were only 86 measles cases in the U.S. The increase in measles cases in recent years has been largely attributed to the decrease in two-dose immunization rates of the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine among U.S. kindergarteners. The target to achieve herd immunity for a population is for 95% to have a two-dose vaccination status, according to the WHO. Throughout the 2010s, two-dose MMR coverage among U.S. kindergarteners was right below this 95% target, and it reached a national high of 95.2% in the 2019-2020 school year. However, beginning in the 2020-2021 school year, two-dose MMR coverage rates fell to 93.9%, and they have continued to decrease since then. For the 2025-2026 school year, the rate was reported to be 92.4%, according to the Centers for Disease Control (CDC). Brewer said this loss of measles elimination status is evidence that the system we had to prevent measles no longer works and that public health education measures need to be taken to fix this. “The nation needs strong, positive communications to the public about the importance of measles, mumps, and rubella vaccination and promotion of health care providers as trusted advisors on immunizations,” Brewer said. He also stated the importance of the government in cultivating public trust in vaccines. “A dedicated communication campaign led by the federal government would be usual in past years at this stage,” he said. “These communications need clear, vocal, and regular support from the highest levels of the U.S. government.” However, the current executive branch administration does not support the CDC’s child vaccination recommendations. U.S. Health and Human Services secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has been leading the anti-vaccination movement in recent years. On Jan. 5, he led the CDC in reducing the number of diseases covered by recommended vaccines on the childhood immunization schedule from 17 to 11. On Mar. 16, this was preliminarily enjoined by the U.S. District Court in Massachusetts in American Academy of Pediatrics v. Kennedy, but the case remains ongoing. Further, on Aug. 10, President Donald Trump published an executive order calling for a change to the current child vaccination recommendations, including splitting the MMR vaccine into three separate vaccines administered at three separate visits. “Together, there could be a possibility they’re quite lethal,” said Trump, according to The Associated Press. “And separately, it looks like they are not at all lethal but just very effective.” In a statement released on Nov. 17, the CDC insisted that its immediate plan as a result of the status change would be to “equip state, local, tribal, and territorial health departments with tools and technical support to identify communities at increased risk, engage trusted partners, detect cases quickly, and respond effectively to outbreaks.” “The measles elimination designation is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does,” said CDC director Dr. Erica Schwartz. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

美国失去 麻疹 消除状态,这是美国健康的历史性挫折 - San Juan Daily Star

US loses its measles elimination status, a historic setback for American health - San Juan Daily Star
新闻简报The United States has lost its measles elimination status, marking a significant setback for public health. This occurred after a resurgence of measles cases began in January 2025, primarily affecting a Mennonite community in Texas. State health officials have reported over 7,700 cases and 10 deaths since then, with hundreds hospitalized, many of them unvaccinated children. The Pan American Health Organization confirmed the loss, stating that the virus has circulated for more than a year, putting families and communities at risk of preventable illness, hospitalization, and death. Experts attribute the resurgence to declining vaccination rates, which have fallen below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity. The CDC has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to address the issue, but regaining elimination status may take many years. The situation highlights the challenges of maintaining public health protections in the face of vaccine hesitancy and misinformation.
查看新闻五要素
时间
美国于2026年失去了麻疹的淘汰状态,并于2025年1月开始复苏。事件日期为2025年1月,发布日期为2026-11-19。
地点与对象
美国,特别是德克萨斯州的门诺派社区,以及更广泛的人群,包括未接种疫苗的儿童、孕妇和新生儿。
事件
由于 麻疹 病例再次出现,美国已经失去了 麻疹 消除状态,该病例自 2025 年 1 月以来大幅增加。泛美卫生组织和州卫生官员证实了这一点。
规模、影响与风险
自 2025 年 1 月以来,已报告超过 7,700 例 麻疹 病例和 10 例死亡。数百人已住院,其中许多未接种疫苗的儿童受到影响。该病毒还传播给了风险较高的孕妇和新生儿。风险被明确表述为可预防的疾病、住院和死亡。
应对、状态与不确定性
The Pan American Health Organization announced the loss of elimination status, and the CDC has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to address the issue. However, experts suggest that regaining elimination status may take many years, and the current status is under investigation.

US loses its measles elimination status, a historic setback for American health The San Juan Daily Star 2 days ago 4 min read A vial of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine at a hospital in Seminole, Texas on Feb. 24, 2025. As vaccination rates have dropped, cases of the once-extinguished measles virus have soared. (Desiree Ríos/The New York Times) By TEDDY ROSENBLUTH Measles has come roaring back in the United States, as vaccination rates have fallen and cases of the once-extinguished virus have soared. That backslide has now cost the nation its measles elimination status, after the virus spread uninterrupted for more than a year. “It’s a giant stain on our reputation,” said Jennifer Nuzzo, director of the Pandemic Center at the Brown University School of Public Health. “Everyone ought to be just utterly disgusted by the willing return back to an age of death and high cost.” The announcement, made Tuesday by the Pan American Health Organization, ushers in a new era in which major outbreaks could routinely disrupt communities around the nation, sickening thousands of children. It also marks a highly embarrassing reversal for a country that showed the rest of the world it was possible to rein in the wildly contagious virus when it won elimination status in 2000. State health officials have reported more than 7,700 cases of measles, including 10 deaths, since the resurgence first kicked off in a Mennonite community in Texas in January 2025. Hundreds have been hospitalized, many of them unvaccinated children. Infections have also risen among pregnant women and newborns, who are at increased risk of death and other dire health consequences. “Losing measles elimination status has broader implications than losing a public health designation,” said Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, director of the Pan American Health Organization, in a news conference announcing the decision. “It means the virus has circulated for a prolonged period of time, putting families and communities at the risk of preventable illness, hospitalization and death.” The organization also announced Tuesday that Bolivia and Mexico had also lost measles elimination status, posing another setback for North America’s handle on the highly contagious virus. Canada officially lost its measles elimination status last year. “None of this had to happen,” said Dr. Peter Hotez, a vaccine scientist and pediatrician at the Baylor College of Medicine. “That’s what’s so tragic.” America was once regarded as a global leader for reining in the highly contagious virus. Now, large outbreaks that sicken thousands could become routine. Chart shows outbreaks since Jan. 2025 by week. The circumstances that have allowed measles to take root again in the United States have been brewing for years, public health experts said. Vaccination rates, which began falling during the COVID-19 pandemic, have continued to drop, and now sit well below the 95% needed to establish herd immunity. In some communities, including many of the insular religious groups hit hardest by measles outbreaks, an even smaller share of children are vaccinated. But experts said under the leadership of Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., federal health officials did too little to control outbreaks early on. And they criticized Kennedy himself for at times directly undermining efforts to improve vaccination rates, by promoting unproven treatments for measles and undermining confidence in childhood vaccines. “I don’t think it’s an accident that this is happening under the current administration,” said Dr. William Moss, an epidemiologist at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health who has studied measles for more than 25 years. Grace Davis Jamison, a spokesperson for the Department of Health and Human Services, said in a statement that the department would continue working to “stop transmission, close vaccination gaps, strengthen outbreak response and protect Americans from measles.” Jamison said that the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention convened a meeting this year to develop a national strategy to regain elimination status. (Losing measles elimination status does not carry any formal penalty.) “We know where transmission is occurring and we’ve invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities,” Dr. Erica Schwartz, the director of the CDC, said in a statement. In a post on the social platform X published Tuesday afternoon, Kennedy added that his department would continue to “support Americans who become ill, regardless of vaccination status, and for the first time equip healthcare providers with guidance to diagnose and treat measles and its complications.” Regaining elimination status is likely to be a monumental task, one that experts said may require a vast public education campaign, investment in on-the-ground immunization efforts and a push to strengthen school vaccination requirements, which several states have loosened in recent years. Even if the CDC and cash-strapped state public health departments make that kind of investment, it will probably be “many years before we get the situation back under control,” said Dr. David Kimberlin, a pediatric infectious disease expert at the University of Alabama at Birmingham. While there were measles cases in the United States between 2000 and 2025, there were fewer than 200 cases most years, and many of them tied to international travel. When the virus did strike an unvaccinated community, the outbreaks did not continue for longer than a year, allowing the country to maintain its status. That meant that many doctors around the country had never seen a case of measles before last year, when more children started arriving at hospitals gasping for breath or experiencing life-threatening brain swelling. Many of those infected since 2025 have been young children, and several of the deaths have been in infants too young to be vaccinated. “We are going to see a growing number of children, as well as young adults, dying from measles,” said Dr. Daniel Salas, who helps lead immunization efforts at the Pan American Health Organization. That is not to mention the long-term health repercussions that are now “baked into our future,” said Dr. Cameron Wolfe, professor of infectious diseases at the Duke University School of Medicine. Even patients who appear to have recovered from measles may fall ill with an entirely different infection months or years later, because the virus weakens the immune system and leaves it more vulnerable to other threats. Since 2025, measles has cost the country roughly $125 million in medical care, lost productivity and public health resources, according to one estimate. Public health experts are hopeful that the country will eventually reverse course, but some fear it will happen only after Americans see death and disability caused by measles in their own communities. “Short of that, I’m not sure how to change the direction of this ship,” Kimberlin said. “That would take a lot of small coffins in the ground.” Recent Posts See All The numbers behind a stunning upset in Mississippi New Yorkers are bracing. For what? They’re not sure. Democrats claim 56 Senate seats after Mary Peltola wins in Alaska bottom of page

According to the Minnesota Department of Health, the largest cluster of measles cases is in Todd County, where 177 cases have been identified. Children under the age of 18 accounted for the majority of these cases. B routine priorityPublic-health news

US loses measles elimination status. Was Alabama affected?

News Brief
The United States lost its measles elimination status in 2026, marking a significant setback in disease control efforts. More than 5,400 cases were reported, with the highest number of infections since 1991. Alabama reported a single case in Huntsville. Health officials attribute the resurgence to declining vaccination rates and are working to increase coverage to prevent further outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Restaurants Real Estate Opinion Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals NEWS US loses measles elimination status. Was Alabama affected? Jennifer Lindahl Montgomery Advertiser Nov. 18, 2026, 1:34 p.m. ET The United States has lost its measles elimination status for the first time since 2000, marking a major setback in the nation’s efforts to control a highly contagious disease that was once considered eliminated. The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) announced Nov. 17 that the United States no longer meets the requirements for measles elimination after the virus continued circulating for more than 12 consecutive months. The decision comes as measles infections have climbed nationwide, with more than 5,400 cases reported in 2026. Alabama has reported relatively few infections compared with states experiencing major outbreaks. However, state health officials confirmed a measles case involving an adult in the Huntsville area. Why did the United States lose its measles elimination status? PAHO determined that the same measles virus lineage had circulated for at least 12 consecutive months, meeting the threshold for losing elimination status. The United States achieved measles elimination status in 2000 after vaccination efforts successfully interrupted continuous domestic transmission of the virus. The designation did not mean measles could never enter the country again. Infections brought in by international travelers could still occur. However, public health officials were able to prevent the virus from spreading continuously within the United States. How many measles cases have been reported in Alabama? The Alabama Department of Public Health confirmed a measles infection involving an adult who lives in the Northern Public Health District. The individual had traveled internationally before testing positive for measles on Oct. 8. Health officials believe the person contracted the virus outside the United States. The CDC’s Nov. 12 national report lists two measles cases in Alabama for 2026, while ADPH’s current information identifies the October case. Which states have the most measles cases in 2026? Measles outbreaks have affected communities across the country, with Pennsylvania, South Carolina, Wisconsin and Utah among the hardest-hit states. According to CDC data updated Nov. 12, 2026, the states reporting the highest numbers of cases include: Pennsylvania: 1,529 cases South Carolina: 670 cases Utah: 536 cases Wisconsin: 505 cases Pennsylvania and Wisconsin have experienced significant increases in infections this year, while South Carolina and Utah have continued to deal with outbreaks that began in 2025. The CDC reported 43 measles outbreaks nationwide in 2026. By comparison, Alabama has reported two cases in the CDC’s national tracking data for 2026. U.S. measles cases reach highest level since 1991 The CDC reported 5,412 confirmed measles cases nationwide as of Nov. 12, 2026. That is more than double the 2,289 cases reported during all of 2025 and a significant increase from the 285 cases recorded in 2024. The 2026 total represents the highest annual number of measles infections in the U.S. since 1991. Health officials have identified declining vaccination rates as a major factor contributing to the resurgence. Declining vaccination rates raise concerns about outbreaks CDC data show that MMR vaccination coverage among kindergarten students declined from 95.2% during the 2019-2020 school year to 92.4% during the 2025-2026 school year. That leaves an estimated 280,000 kindergarten students without the protection provided by the recommended vaccination coverage. Health officials generally recommend that at least 95% of a community be vaccinated to help prevent widespread measles transmission. The measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine is approximately 97% effective against measles after two doses. What is measles and what are the symptoms Measles is a highly contagious viral disease that spreads through the air when an infected person coughs or sneezes. The virus can remain infectious in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves an area. People without immunity can become infected simply by entering a contaminated space. Symptoms typically develop seven to 14 days after exposure and include high fever, cough, runny nose and red, watery eyes. A red rash usually begins on the face before spreading to the rest of the body. Small white spots may also appear inside the mouth. Although many people recover, measles can cause serious complications, including pneumonia, ear infections and inflammation of the brain. Young children, especially those younger than 5, face an increased risk of severe illness. Jennifer Lindahl is a Breaking and Trending Reporter in Alabama for USA TODAY’s Deep South Connect Team. B routine priorityPublic-health news

US loses measles elimination status as global cases rise - Down To Earth

News Brief
The US has lost its measles-elimination status, joining Mexico and Bolivia, as the Americas and Europe face a resurgence of measles cases. The disease is highly contagious and can cause severe complications, particularly in young children. With 58,213 confirmed cases and 82 deaths in the Americas as of November 13, 2026, the situation highlights the need for increased vaccination rates and improved access to healthcare to prevent further outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
हिंदी മലയാളം Print Edition Support Us Sign in Subscribe Health Agriculture Water Waste Climate Air Africa Data Centre Video Gallery Food Bookshop CSE Bulletins Subscribe Health Agriculture Water Waste Climate Air Africa Data Centre Video Gallery Food Bookshop CSE Bulletins Health US loses measles elimination status as global cases rise Declining childhood vaccination rates, pandemic-related immunity gaps and unequal access to healthcare are fuelling measles outbreaks, threatening decades of progress against a preventable disease A woman with a red, spotty rash on her upper back and shoulders caused by measles.Credit: efired / istockphoto Snigdha Das Published on: 18 Nov 2026, 3:16 am Copied Listen to this article Summary The US has lost its measles-elimination status after 12 months of sustained transmission. Mexico and Bolivia have also lost theirs, joining Canada. Measles is highly contagious and potentially fatal, causing complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, particularly in young children. Two vaccine doses and 95 per cent population coverage are needed to preventsustainedtransmission. Falling vaccination rates are fuelling the global resurgence, driven by pandemic-related immunisationgaps, rising vaccine exemptions and unequal access to healthcare. The US has officially lost its measles-elimination status, reversing one of its most significant public-health achievements. On November 17, Dr Jarbas Barbosa, director of the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), the regional arm of the World Health Organization (WHO), confirmed that the country had failed to contain sustained transmission of the highly contagious disease. Mexico and Bolivia have also lost their elimination status, joining Canada, which was stripped of the distinction last year. The development marks a setback for the Americas, which became the first WHO region to eliminate measles in 2016. The US had achieved elimination in 2000, becoming the first country in the world to do so. For the next 26 years, the disease remained largely under control, with occasional imported cases and local outbreaks failing to establish sustained transmission. Under PAHO's criteria, measles elimination status is lost when the same measles virus genotype and lineage circulate uninterrupted for 12 months or more within a country. "Losing measles elimination status is much more than losing a public-health designation. It means the virus has circulated for a prolonged period, putting families and communities at risk of preventable illness, hospitalisation and death," said Dr Barbosa in a media briefing. As of November 13, 2026, the Americas had reported 58,213 confirmed measles cases and 82 deaths, compared with 12,596 cases during the same period in 2025, Dr Barbosa said. Also Read Measles can ravage the immune system and brain, causing long-term damage — a virologist explains The resurgence is not confined to the Americas. In January 2026, the WHO announced that six countries in its European Region—Armenia, Austria, Azerbaijan, Spain, the UK and Uzbekistan—had lost their measles-elimination status. The decisions were made by the European Regional Verification Commission in September 2025, based on epidemiological data from 2024 showing that endemic measles transmission had been re-established in these countries. The developments underscore the reversal of one of the greatest public health achievements this century. What is measles and how does it spread? Measles is one of the world's most contagious infectious diseases. It spreads primarily through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes or talks, releasing virus-containing respiratory particles. The virus can remain infectious in the air or on contaminated surfaces for up to two hours. An infected person can transmit the virus to as many as 18 susceptible individuals, making measles considerably more contagious than many other infectious diseases. Transmission can occur from four days before the rash appears to four days afterwards. The disease can affect people of any age, although children are particularly vulnerable, especially those who are unvaccinated or have weakened immune systems. According to the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, measles can cause complications including ear infections, diarrhoea and pneumonia. In more severe cases, it can lead to encephalitis, an inflammation of the brain that may result in permanent brain damage. Severe infections can require hospitalisation and can be fatal. Symptoms generally appear seven to 14 days after exposure to the virus. The disease typically begins with fever, a runny nose, cough and red, watery eyes. A characteristic red rash generally appears three to five days after the initial symptoms, beginning on the face and upper neck before spreading downwards to the trunk, arms, legs and feet. The rash typically spreads over about three days and lasts four to eight days before gradually fading. Although the rash is the most recognisable sign of measles, the disease can cause serious complications, particularly in young children, malnourished individuals and people with weakened immunity. Also Read We study pandemics, and the resurgence of measles is a grim sign of what’s coming Vaccination remains the most effective protection There is no specific antiviral treatment for measles. Vaccination is the most effective way to prevent measles and control outbreaks. According to the WHO, before the introduction of the measles vaccine in 1963 and the expansion of immunisation programmes, major epidemics occurred approximately every two to three years, causing an estimated 2.6 million deaths annually. Accelerated immunisation activities by countries, the WHO, the Measles and Rubella Partnership and other international partners prevented an estimated 59 million deaths between 2000 and 2024; annual measles deaths reduced from 780,000 in 2000 to 95,000 in 2024. Two doses are recommended to provide reliable immunity. Not all children develop sufficient protection after the first dose, making the second essential for closing immunity gaps and preventing outbreaks. In countries where measles is common, the first dose is generally administered at nine months of age. In other countries, it is usually given between 12 and 15 months. A second dose is administered later in childhood, with the timing varying across national programmes. Epidemiologists estimate that at least 95 per cent of the population needs to be fully vaccinated with two doses to prevent sustained transmission and maintain community-level protection. Such high community-wide vaccination coverage not only protects immunised individuals but also helps prevent transmission to those who cannot receive the vaccine, including young infants and some people undergoing cancer treatment. However, recent increases in transmission demonstrate how quickly progress can be undermined when immunisation coverage falls. Also Read 43% increase in measles deaths from 2021-2022: WHO Falling childhood vaccination rates weakening protection Despite the availability of a safe and cost-effective vaccine, WHO estimates that approximately 95,000 people died from measles in 2024, most of them unvaccinated or under-vaccinated children under five. The disease remains particularly prevalent in parts of Africa, Asia and the Eastern Mediterranean, where vaccination coverage is insufficient and access to health services can be limited. According to WHO, global vaccination coverage remains below the levels needed to prevent outbreaks. In 2025, approximately 84 per cent of children received their first dose of measles vaccine and 77 per cent received a complete vaccination course. PAHO also states that declining childhood immunisation rates are a major factor behind the return of sustained transmission in several countries. Also Read Measles could again become widespread as cases surge worldwide In the US, although nearly every state and Washington, DC, requires children to receive both doses before attending public school, exemptions are permitted. Medical exemptions are available for children with certain health conditions, while some states also allow exemptions on religious or personal-belief grounds. The number of children receiving non-medical vaccine exemptions has increased in recent years, while routine vaccination coverage has declined. During the 2024-25 school year, MMR vaccination (combination immunisation against meales, mumps and rubella) coverage among American kindergarten children fell to 92.5 per cent, according to CDC data. This was below the 95 per cent threshold considered necessary to prevent sustained measles transmission and represented the lowest coverage recorded since 2000. The shortfall leaves a growing proportion of children susceptible to infection. When vaccination coverage falls, measles can spread rapidly through schools and communities, particularly among unvaccinated children and those too young to receive the vaccine. The WHO identifies several overlapping factors behind the resurgence of measles, including declining vaccination coverage in countries that have failed to maintain the high two-dose coverage needed to prevent outbreaks, and growing immunity gaps caused by children missing routine vaccinations during the COVID-19 pandemic and in subsequent years. covid-19 The World Health Organization Vaccination Measles MMR Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) Related Stories School of change Bhagirath 14 Nov 2026 The choking games we play Ritu Rao 07 Nov 2026 Daily Court Digest: Major environment orders (October 22, 2026) Susan Chacko 22 Oct 2026 Climate crisis also a crisis of the mind Shruti Sharma 10 Oct 2026 Do Not Sell or Share My Personal Information Opt out of the sale or sharing of personal information We won't sell or share your personal information to inform the ads you see. Time
Mexico and Bolivia have also lost theirs, joining Canada.
Location and population
US, Mexico, Bolivia, Canada, and other countries in the Americas and Europe.
Event
Measles is highly contagious and potentially fatal, causing complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, particularly in young children.
Scale, impact and risk
58,213 confirmed measles cases and 82 deaths in the Americas as of November 13, 2026, compared to 12,596 cases in the same period in 2025. Two doses of the vaccine and 95% population coverage are needed to prevent sustained transmission.
Response, status and uncertainty
The US and other countries are facing declining childhood vaccination rates, pandemic-related immunity gaps, and unequal access to healthcare, which are fuelling measles outbreaks. The situation is under investigation and requires urgent action to prevent further spread.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

U.S. Loses Measles Elimination Status After 26 Years as Outbreaks Surge Nationwide - Rolling Out

News Brief
The United States has lost its measles elimination status, marking a significant public health setback after 26 years of success. The decision is based on ongoing measles outbreaks and thousands of confirmed cases, with 5,412 cases reported as of November 12, 2026. Measles is highly contagious, with one infected person potentially spreading the virus to up to 18 susceptible individuals. The disease can cause serious complications including pneumonia, brain inflammation, blindness, and death. Health officials are urging more aggressive responses to outbreaks and stronger vaccination programs.
Original source text (English)
Opens in a new tab News Entertainment Culture Business Health IQ Beauty Videos Search NEWS All NewsPoliticsCrimeCommunity NewsObituariesJustice For All ENTERTAINMENT All EntertainmentTVMoviesPhotosMusicCoversSportsReality CheckCars CULTURE All CultureFashionTravelRelationshipsCocktail & BeerCreative Lens BUSINESS All BusinessBlack IntellectualsCEO to CEODiversity Equity & FocusSisters with SuperpowersHome Ownership & Real EstateEntrepreneurs & Business LeadersExecutive SuiteFinance & WealthMarketing & Branding HEALTH IQ Health IQBe the Match AtlantaFitnessFood & Nutrition BEAUTY All Beauty VIDEOS All Videos EXPERIENCES Browse Places & Events HŌTEL Visit Hōtel FEATURES HBCU CultureShop NewsletterPrivacy Policy Home » News U.S. Loses Measles Elimination Status After 26 Years as Outbreaks Surge Nationwide The decision means the US can no longer claim that measles transmission has been eliminated By Terry Shropshire Nov 18, 2026 6:58 pm (Image source: Youtube/ABC News) The United States has officially lost its measles elimination status for the first time in 26 years, marking a troubling reversal in one of the country’s most significant public health achievements. The announcement, made November 17, 2026, by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), comes as measles outbreaks continue spreading across the country, with thousands of confirmed infections reported this year. The decision means the United States can no longer claim that measles transmission has been eliminated within its borders. Instead, the virus is considered endemic again, signaling that it has circulated continuously for at least 12 months. A Historic Public Health Achievement Reversed The United States first achieved measles elimination status in 2000 after years of successful vaccination campaigns and public health initiatives dramatically reduced the disease’s spread. However, that progress has been undermined by recurring outbreaks, declining vaccination coverage and pockets of communities where immunization rates remain below recommended levels. PAHO Director Dr. Jarbas Barbosa confirmed that an international monitoring commission recommended withdrawing the country’s elimination designation after determining that the same measles virus lineage had circulated uninterrupted for at least 12 months. “Losing measles elimination status is much more than losing a public health designation. It means the virus has circulated for a prolonged period, putting families and communities at risk of preventable illness, hospitalization and death,” Barbosa said, according to the Associated Press. The United States is not alone in losing its designation. Bolivia and Mexico also lost their measles elimination status following sustained transmission. Canada previously lost its designation in 2025. According to PAHO, the decision underscores the growing challenge of controlling measles throughout the Americas. Thousands of Measles Cases Raise Alarm The loss of elimination status comes during the country’s worst measles resurgence in more than three decades. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), the United States recorded 5,412 confirmed measles cases in 2026 as of November 12, following nearly 2,300 cases in 2025. More than 7,000 cases have been reported since the outbreak that began in West Texas in 2025. The overwhelming majority of infections have involved people who were unvaccinated or whose vaccination status was unknown. Measles is among the most contagious infectious diseases in the world. One infected person can spread the virus to as many as 18 susceptible individuals, making outbreaks particularly difficult to contain in communities with low vaccination coverage. Symptoms typically include fever, cough, runny nose, red and watery eyes, and a distinctive rash that usually begins on the face before spreading across the body. Although some people recover without serious complications, measles can cause pneumonia, brain inflammation, blindness and, in severe cases, death. The infection can also weaken the immune system for months afterward. Declining Vaccination Rates Fuel Concern Health officials point to declining childhood vaccination rates as a significant factor behind the resurgence. The measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine remains the most effective protection against the disease. Public health experts generally recommend that at least 95% of a population receive two doses to maintain community protection and prevent sustained transmission. However, CDC data show that only 92.4% of U.S. kindergartners received their required MMR vaccinations during the 2025-2026 school year, compared with 95.2% during the 2019-2020 school year. That decline leaves communities increasingly vulnerable, particularly where vaccination rates are substantially lower than the national average. The CDC has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to help reach communities experiencing outbreaks and close vaccination gaps. The agency also pledged to work with healthcare providers, laboratories and community organizations to strengthen surveillance, improve vaccine access and respond more quickly to new infections. RFK Jr. Pledges to Restore Measles Elimination The announcement also places renewed attention on the federal government’s handling of vaccine policy under Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. Kennedy, who has faced criticism over his history of vaccine skepticism, said the administration intends to stop measles outbreaks and restore the country’s elimination status. He has continued to recommend the MMR vaccine while pointing to disruptions in routine childhood immunization during the COVID-19 pandemic as one contributor to declining protection. The agency also reported that several states, including Texas, South Carolina, Virginia, Kansas, New Mexico and Wisconsin, have requested federal assistance to contain outbreaks. Public Health Experts Call for Urgent Action PAHO is urging governments throughout the Americas to strengthen vaccination programs, improve disease surveillance and respond more aggressively to outbreaks. As of November 13, 2026, the region had recorded 58,213 confirmed measles cases and 82 deaths, compared with 12,806 cases during the same period in 2025. Barbosa warned that the situation is serious but not irreversible. “This is a serious warning for our Region, but it is not an irreversible setback,” he said. “We know how to prevent measles, and we know how to stop its transmission.” Recommended Caleb Williams outsells Josh Allen to claim NFL’s top-selling jersey Why is Congress threatening SpaceX over Starshield? Chris Tucker in Talks to Return as Smokey in Ice Cube’s ‘Last Friday’ Movie in 2027 Is Nick Cannon okay after his ‘Pray for me’ post? Coastal flood watch issued for California central coast as waves surge up to 22 feet Steps on Netflix flips Cinderella, so is it worth it? Capitol Fax.com - Your Illinois News Radar » US loses measles elimination status, but Illinois has so far avoided major outbreaks SUBSCRIBE to Capitol Fax Advertise Here About Exclusive Subscriber Content Updated Posts Contact Rich Miller CapitolFax.com To subscribe to Capitol Fax, click here. US loses measles elimination status, but Illinois has so far avoided major outbreaks Tuesday, Nov 17, 2026 - Posted by Isabel Miller * The New York Times... Measles has come roaring back in the United States, as vaccination rates have fallen and cases of the once-extinguished virus have soared. That backslide has now cost the nation its measles elimination status, after the virus spread uninterrupted for more than a year. “It’s a giant stain on our reputation,” said Jennifer Nuzzo, director of the Pandemic Center at the Brown University School of Public Health. “Everyone ought to be just utterly disgusted by the willing return back to an age of death and high cost.” The announcement, made Tuesday by the Pan American Health Organization, ushers in a new era in which major outbreaks could routinely disrupt communities around the nation, sickening thousands of children. It also marks a highly embarrassing reversal for a country that showed the rest of the world it was possible to rein in the wildly contagious virus when it won elimination status in 2000. State health officials have reported more than 7,700 cases of measles, including 10 deaths, since the resurgence first kicked off in a Mennonite community in Texas in January 2025. Hundreds have been hospitalized, many of them unvaccinated children. Infections have also risen among pregnant women and newborns, who are at increased risk of death and other dire health consequences. * The Sun-Times... While outbreaks have pummeled several states, Illinois continues to see a low count of measles cases. So far in 2026, 29 cases have been reported to the Illinois Department of Public Health. No one has died from the virus or been hospitalized this year. Cook County has only one reported case so far this year. Downstate Douglas County has reported the most with 11 cases. But in previous years, Illinois has reported single-digit case counts or no cases at all. [...] Illinois’ rate for the two-dose measles vaccine is holding steady among school-aged children, according to data from the Illinois State Board of Education. For the 2025-2026 school year, the state’s vaccination rate is 96.8%. Chicago Public Schools rate is 95.7%, an improvement after the district saw a dip in vaccinations coming out of the COVID-19 pandemic. [...] In the Chicago area, “we still do a great job of having most of our children get the two doses of the vaccine,” [Dr. Ravi Jhaveri, the division head of pediatric infectious diseases at Lurie Children’s Hospital,] said. “The risk for us locally is not that high. There will still be small clusters, small outbreaks.” * The county-by-county breakdown from the Illinois Department of Public Health... * Related... * WaPo | CDC declined to alert risk of measles in pregnancy. A journal released the data: The analysis said 112 pregnant women had measles during the 21 months from January 2025 through Sept. 28 of this year. The report found: Twenty-six of the pregnant women with measles were hospitalized for reasons other than labor, although the reason for hospitalization was not reported in every case. Eleven developed pneumonia, two experienced respiratory failure, and one was admitted to intensive care. [...] The analysis provides some of the first contemporary U.S. data on measles during pregnancy in decades and the first from the current outbreak. * NYT | What It Took for America to Wipe Out Measles: There is no known disease more contagious than measles. It can linger for hours in the air and people can spread it days before realizing they are sick. “The measles virus is relentless,” said Dr. Daniel Salas, who helps lead immunization efforts at the Pan American Health Organization, the organization responsible for reviewing the country’s elimination status. “It doesn’t forgive any kind of window of opportunity.” * Politico | In America’s measles epidemic, RFK Jr. is now on trial: Under Kennedy and CDC Director Erica Schwartz, the government “has mounted an aggressive response to measles outbreaks across the country,” HHS spokesperson Emily Hilliard said in a statement, noting the agency deployed experts to help states battle outbreaks and put $100 million in additional funding toward the effort.The two leaders recommend vaccination “as the most effective way to prevent measles,” Hilliard said. But this summer, as measles cases rose, President Donald Trump signed an executive order urging drugmakers to break up the combination shot that provides protection against measles, mumps and rubella, suggesting it was “quite lethal.” The order also directed the attorney general to go after states that don’t allow religious exemptions to vaccines. Public health advocates say the administration’s mixed messaging on vaccine safety and the insistence that shots are a “personal” choice are making the situation worse. 24 Comments » - Norseman - Tuesday, Nov 17, 26 @ 11:11 am: IL is a blue state where the leadership continues to support health expertise. 病例数创纪录,美国时隔 26 年失去 麻疹 消除地位 - BBC

US loses measles elimination status after 26 years amid record case numbers - BBC
新闻简报In 2026, the United States lost its measles elimination status after 26 years, marking a significant setback in public health. This decision was made by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), which determined that the measles virus had circulated continuously for over a year. The CDC confirmed the loss and emphasized its commitment to regaining elimination status through expanded vaccine access and improved public health measures. The US reported 5,400 measles cases and 10 deaths in 2026, the highest since the 1990s, with Pennsylvania experiencing an outbreak that infected over 1,400 people and killed seven. The vaccination rate for the MMR vaccine dropped to 92.4%, below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity. Public health officials and experts attribute the decline in vaccination rates to misinformation and skepticism, particularly from high-profile figures like Robert F Kennedy Jr and Donald Trump. The CDC has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to address these gaps, but some deaths from the Pennsylvania outbreak remain unrecorded in its database. The situation highlights the ongoing challenges in maintaining measles control and the importance of public trust in vaccination programs.
查看新闻五要素
时间
美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 和泛美卫生组织 (PAHO) 报告称,麻疹 将于 2026 年失去消除状态,CDC 注意到了截至公告时的情况。2026 年,宾夕法尼亚州也报道了 暴发 疫情,该疫情导致 1,400 多人感染并导致 7 人死亡。2026年,麻疹病例数达到35年来的新高。
地点与对象
美国,特别是宾夕法尼亚州,经历了一次大的 麻疹 暴发。整个美国人口都受到影响,全国各地都有 麻疹 病例报告。
事件
由于创纪录的病例数和死亡人数,美国在 26 年后失去了 麻疹 消除状态。泛美卫生组织(PAHO)确定该病毒已不间断传播一年多,标志着该国无 麻疹 状态的结束。疾病预防控制中心确认了这一损失,并表示致力于恢复消除状态。
规模、影响与风险
美国在2026年报告了5400例麻疹病例,其中10例与麻疹相关死亡,这是自1990年代以来的最高记录。宾夕法尼亚州的暴发感染了超过1400人,导致7人死亡。CDC指出,MMR的疫苗接种率降至92.4%,低于所需的95% herd immunity阈值。麻疹再次暴发的风险被强调为该地区的一个严重警告。
应对、状态与不确定性
The CDC and its partners are committed to stopping measles outbreaks and regaining elimination status. They have invested $100 million in state and local health departments to address vaccination gaps. However, the CDC has not yet recorded some of the deaths from Pennsylvania in its database. The US Health Secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, has attempted to change vaccine recommendations and policies, but these efforts have been met with resistance and legal challenges.

US loses measles elimination status after 26 years amid record case numbers - BBC

News Brief
In 2026, the United States lost its measles elimination status after 26 years, marking a significant setback in public health. This decision was made by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), which determined that the measles virus had circulated continuously for over a year. The CDC confirmed the loss and emphasized its commitment to regaining elimination status through expanded vaccine access and improved public health measures. The US reported 5,400 measles cases and 10 deaths in 2026, the highest since the 1990s, with Pennsylvania experiencing an outbreak that infected over 1,400 people and killed seven. The situation highlights the ongoing challenges in maintaining measles control and the importance of public trust in vaccination programs.

After more than two decades, the US has lost its measles-free status, following two years of record case numbers and deaths. The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), a specialised service of the United Nations, determined the US has had uninterrupted transmission of the same strain of measles for more than 12 months. Dr Jarbas Barbosa, director of PAHO, announced that Mexico and Bolivia have also lost their measles elimination status, attributing the resurgence in the region to persistent "gaps in vaccination". The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) said it was committed to "regaining measles elimination status" by expanding access to vaccines. The US has seen 5,400 cases of measles this year alone, with 10 measles-related deaths, the most the country has seen since the 1990s. The measles virus - with its signature blotchy red rash - used to infect millions of children a year before a vaccine was invented. The extremely contagious virus can be fatal, and some patients can experience long-term complications including encephalitis and immune amnesia, which resets the immune system, leaving it with only limited ability to respond to new infections. The US recommends two measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) shots for children between ages one and six, which is 97% effective in protecting against the illness. PAHO, a group within the World Health Organization, revoked the US's measles-elimination after determining that the virus had circulated nonstop for more than a year. "These figures are a serious warning for our region," PAHO's director Barbosa said at a news conference on Tuesday. "Measles is resurging globally amid persistent gaps in vaccination." He added that Canada, which lost its status of having eliminated measles last year after seeing several large outbreaks, has made strides this year to interrupt transmission. Daniel Salas, the executive manager of immunisations at PAHO, said that the US and others who lost elimination status would be required to submit corrective action plans outlining how they would attempt to improve vaccination rates. US Health Secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr Kennedy has attempted to make a series of sweeping changes to US vaccine recommendations and policies. The vaccine sceptic has also frequently cast doubt on the safety of MMR shot, as vaccination rates have declined across the US in recent years. Kennedy has argued that the rise in measles cases started before he took the helm at HHS and that neighbouring countries have also seen more infections. Responding to Tuesday's announcement, the CDC said the agency and its partners were "committed to stopping measles outbreaks, protecting Americans, and regaining measles elimination status". "Together, we will encourage MMR vaccination, expand access to vaccines, and help communities close gaps in protection," the agency said. CDC Director Erica Schwartz said the measles elimination designation was not what protected Americans. "Vaccination does," she said. "We know where transmission is occurring and we've invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities." In a post on social media, Kennedy said: "I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles." "We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust," he also said. "We will answer families' questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment." The UK, Spain and Austria were among European nations that lost their measles-free status earlier this year, along with countries including Russia, Azerbaijan and Uzbekistan. Nuwan Gunawardhana, an assistant professor of medicine with Columbia University Irving Medical Center, said the US losing its elimination status was "a huge leap backwards". The US was the first country to eliminate measles in the Americas thanks to vaccination campaigns, noted William Moss, a paediatric infectious disease specialist at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health. Dr Gunawardhana said it took massive efforts from public health officials and clinicians to build trust with patients to increase uptake of the MMR vaccine, leading to decades in the US with few infections and deaths. But that trust has been steadily eroding since the Covid-19 pandemic, when Dr Gunawardhana said patients were confronted with misinformation about the safety of vaccines and lost even more trust in public health systems. The vaccination rate for MMR across the US has fallen to 92.4%, below the 95% level that provides herd immunity, which limits the spread of measles and protects those who cannot get vaccinated, including newborns. Getty Images The vaccination rate for MMR across the US has fallen to 92.4%, below the 95% level that provides herd immunity - which limits the spread of measles An outbreak in Pennsylvania has infected over 1,400 people - likely a large underestimate - and killed seven people, according to state officials. Kennedy, the health secretary, has sparred with Pennsylvania officials over how it is counting the measles deaths, with CDC declining for now to record some of the deaths in its database. Last year, the health secretary fired all the members of a top vaccine advisory panel and replaced them with his own appointees, some of whom were vaccine critics. The panel recommended a number of vaccine changes - including no longer requiring the Hepatitis B vaccine for all newborns - but those recommendations were blocked by a federal judge. President Donald Trump this year also cast doubt on the safety of the MMR vaccine, issuing an executive order for the shot to be split into three separate immunisations for measles, mumps and rubella, a move public health experts say is costly and could sow confusion about an already safe vaccine. Doctors and public health officials say the misinformation from Kennedy and Trump has played a major role in falling vaccination rates. "It's confusing patients. It's confusing families," Dr Gunawardhana said. "We need a clear message from the top." The federal government's vaccine manoeuvres have left public health officials and states to fill in the gaps, offering their own separate recommendations upholding decades-long practices. Georgia sees record spike in confirmed measles cases as U.S. grapples with outbreaks - CBS News

News Brief
Georgia has experienced a significant increase in measles cases in 2026, with 28 confirmed cases across the state, nearly triple the number from 2025 and 10 more than in 2019. This surge coincides with the U.S. losing its measles elimination status, as the CDC reported nearly 2,300 cases and 43 outbreaks nationwide. Most cases involve unvaccinated individuals, with only 4% of confirmed cases having at least one dose of the MMR vaccine. At least six deaths have been linked to the virus, with additional fatalities reported in Pennsylvania, Wisconsin, and New York. Georgia officials are urging vaccination, emphasizing the safety and effectiveness of the MMR vaccine, which provides immunity to over 95% of recipients with one dose and 98% with two. The measles virus is highly contagious, spreading through the air and surviving on surfaces for up to two hours. The outbreak in Macon County is connected to a confirmed case in Pennsylvania, with the individual having visited a LongHorn Steakhouse on Oct. 7 while infectious. Other outbreaks have been confirmed in Jasper County and Cobb County. The CDC has confirmed the spread of measles, but details about the original case remain limited.

Read Full Bio Dan Raby November 20, 2026 / 12:42 PM EST / CBS Atlanta Add CBS News on Google Georgia has seen more measles cases in 2026 than any other year since the state's Department of Public Health began tracking the annual number of cases. The spike in cases in the Peach State comes as the Pan American Health Organization announced the United States has officially lost its measles elimination status. International health officials consider measles eliminated when the virus is not locally spreading among residents. The designation hinges on whether a single measles chain has spread uninterrupted for at least 12 months. The United States has met that standard every year since 2000. As of Thursday, the Atlanta-based Centers for Disease Control and Prevention have confirmed nearly 2,300 measles cases in the country with 43 new outbreaks (defined as three or more cases) this year. The biggest outbreak has been in Pennsylvania, where more than 1,500 cases have been confirmed. The measles virus, Paramyxoviridae from the Morbillivirus family, transmission microscopy view. BSIP/Universal Images Group via Getty Images The CDC data shows there have been at least six deaths connected with the virus, but the agency noted those numbers are likely to increase. CBS News reports that there have been eight deaths associated with measles in Pennsylvania alone, with other deaths reported in Wisconsin and New York. In Georgia, health officials have confirmed 28 cases of measles across the state, nearly triple the number reported in 2025 and 10 more than in 2019, which was the highest number of cases since the state started keeping records in 2002. The latest outbreak was in Macon County, where DPH confirmed three measles cases on Nov. 10. So far, there have been seven cases connected with the outbreak. While details about the original case remain limited, officials say he or she had contact with a confirmed measles case in Pennsylvania. Authorities believe the individual also visited a LongHorn Steakhouse on Hampton Court in Perry on the night of Oct. 7 while they were infectious. Other outbreaks in the state have been confirmed in Jasper County and Cobb County. Mexico also lost its measles-elimination status, following a resurgence of the virus there that included a massive outbreak in Chihuahua state. Bolivia, too, lost its status. Last year, Canada lost its measles-free designation for the first time since 1998. Georgia officials urge measles vaccinations The majority of cases across the country have been unvaccinated or had unknown vaccination status, the CDC reports. Only 4% of confirmed cases had at least one dose of the MMR vaccine. In a letter sent to physicians across the state in September, Georgia Department of Public Health Commissioner Dr. Kathleen E. Toomey asked doctors to encourage their patients to get vaccinated. "The MMR (measles, mumps, and rubella) vaccine is safe and highly effective. More than 95% of people who receive one dose develop immunity, and a second dose increases protection to approximately 98%. Vaccination also helps protect infants and others who cannot be vaccinated," Toomey wrote. A vial of the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine for children is displayed during an immunization event at the L.A. Care and Blue Shield of California Promise Health Plan Community Resource Center in the Panorama City neighborhood of Los Angeles on Oct. 24, 2025. PATRICK T. FALLON/AFP via Getty Images What are the symptoms of measles? Measles first infects the respiratory tract, then spreads throughout the body, causing a high fever, runny nose, cough, red, watery eyes, and a rash. The rash generally appears three to five days after the first symptoms, beginning as flat red spots on the face and then spreading downward to the neck, trunk, arms, legs, and feet. When the rash appears, the fever may spike over 104 degrees Fahrenheit, according to the CDC. The virus is highly contagious, spreads through the air, and can stay on surfaces for up to two hours after an infected person has been in a room. The Associated Press contributed to this report. B routine priorityPublic-health news

USA Loses Measles Elimination Status After 26 Years: Cases and Deaths Surge

News Brief
The United States has lost its measles elimination status after 26 years, with a major resurgence of measles cases and deaths. As of November 13, 2026, there were 5,412 confirmed cases in 47 US jurisdictions, with 43 reported outbreaks. The resurgence is linked to declining childhood vaccination coverage, particularly among kindergarteners, where MMR vaccination coverage dropped from 95.2% to 92.4%. Public health authorities are focusing on improving vaccination rates, identifying low-immunization communities, and strengthening surveillance to regain elimination status.
