MEDINDIA MEDINDIA Consult Online Doctor Courses Explore Healthy Living News Health A-Z Calculators Articles Drugs Directories Education More Search Medindia Medindia » News » Research News » USA Loses Measles Elimination Status After 26 Years: Cases and Deaths Surge USA Loses Measles Elimination Status After 26 Years: Cases and Deaths Surge Written by Colleen Fleiss Reviewed by The Medindia News Review Team on Nov 15 2026 11:45 PM Add MedIndia on Google Set MedIndia as a preferred source on Google to consistently get trusted, expert-backed health and wellness content in your search results. Why has the USA lost its measles elimination status after 26 years? Discover what is driving the outbreak, rising measles cases and the warning signs. The United States has lost a major public health achievement after measles regained a foothold in the country, ending its 26-year record of measles elimination. The development marks a significant setback for American public health authorities, who had maintained measles elimination status since 2000 through vaccination programmes, disease surveillance and outbreak-control measures. According to reports published on November 13 and 14, 2026, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) determined that the United States no longer met the criteria for measles elimination after prolonged transmission of the virus. (1✔) The decision comes amid a major resurgence of measles across the country, with thousands of confirmed infections and additional deaths reported in 2026. The development also raises questions about declining childhood vaccination coverage and the ability of public health systems to prevent the return of diseases once considered eliminated. Measles Measles is a viral infection with symptoms of fever,and rashes. Measles is prevented by vaccination and staying away from the infected individuals. Why Did the United States Lose Its Measles Elimination Status? The United States first achieved measles elimination in 2000. However, elimination does not mean that the virus has disappeared worldwide or that imported cases cannot occur. It means that continuous local transmission of measles has been interrupted. According to PAHO, the assessment considers whether the same measles virus strain has continued circulating within a country for at least 12 months. The current US outbreak began in January 2025. Despite public health interventions, transmission continued through multiple outbreaks, allowing the virus to establish sustained circulation. In January 2026, PAHO announced that the United States and Mexico would undergo a review of their measles elimination status following outbreaks that began in 2025. The subsequent review found that the United States had lost its elimination status. This is particularly significant because the country had maintained that achievement for more than two decades. Test Your Knowledge on Measles Measles, also known as morbilli or rubeola or red measles, is a highly contagious, rash-causing infection belonging to paramyxovirus family. It also causes upper respiratory symptoms and fever. Efforts are being made to eradicate measles from the ... What Do US Government Health Figures Reveal About the Outbreak? The latest figures from the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) show how extensively measles has spread. According to the CDC's November 13, 2026 update: (2✔) 5,412 Confirmed measles cases in 2026 47 US jurisdictions reporting cases 43 Reported measles outbreaks in 2026 96% Cases associated with outbreaks Source: CDC surveillance data as of November 12, 2026. The figures represent a substantial increase compared with previous years. The country reported 2,289 confirmed measles cases in 2025, compared with just 285 in 2024. Measles Outbreak in Texas: 597 Cases, 62 Hospitalizations, 2 Deaths How long is measles contagious? From 4 days before to 4 days after the rash. Stay informed and protected during this Texas outbreak. Measles Symptoms and Treatment: Early Signs, Rash and Available Care Measles usually begins with a high fever, cough, runny nose and red, watery eyes. Small white spots called Koplik spots may appear inside the mouth before the characteristic red rash develops. The rash starts on the face and hairline before spreading downwards to the neck, trunk, arms and legs. Symptoms generally appear 7–14 days after exposure. (3✔) There is no specific antiviral treatment for routine measles infection. Management focuses on supportive care, including adequate fluids, rest and fever management. Vitamin A supplementation may be recommended for children with measles under medical supervision, particularly in severe cases. Antibiotics are not effective against the measles virus but may be prescribed if secondary bacterial infections develop. Severe complications, such as pneumonia or encephalitis, may require hospitalization and specialized medical care. Anyone suspected of having measles should contact a healthcare provider before visiting a medical facility to prevent further transmission. Measles Resurgence: Tackling Viral Outbreak As compared to previous years, India's capital has seen a sharp increase in measles cases, with 543 cases reported in 2022. Falling MMR Vaccination Coverage Adds to the Concern One of the most important factors behind the American measles resurgence is declining childhood vaccination coverage. The CDC reports that MMR vaccination coverage among US kindergarteners dropped from 95.2% during the 2019–2020 school year to 92.4% in 2025–2026. The decline means approximately 280,000 kindergarteners were potentially at risk during the 2025–2026 school year. (4✔) The CDC explains that measles outbreaks can spread rapidly when the virus enters communities with lower vaccination coverage. Although 95% coverage is an important community-immunity target, local vaccination gaps can still allow outbreaks even when national coverage appears relatively high. The agency also points to increased measles activity globally, which creates more opportunities for infected travelers to bring the virus into the United States. What Happens Next? Can the USA Regain Measles Elimination Status? Losing elimination status does not mean that the United States cannot control measles again. The country would need to interrupt sustained local transmission and demonstrate that endemic transmission has stopped. The PAHO review highlights the importance of: (5✔) Improving childhood MMR vaccination coverage. Identifying communities with low immunization rates. Strengthening disease surveillance and laboratory testing. Quickly identifying and containing new outbreaks. Improving public awareness about vaccine-preventable diseases. The CDC continues to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective protection against measles. The loss of measles elimination status in the USA is a reminder that decades of public health progress can be reversed when vaccination gaps widen, making sustained immunization, early detection and effective outbreak control essential to keeping measles at bay. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Virginia Health Department confirms measles case in Central Virginia

News Brief
The Virginia Department of Health confirmed a measles case in a child under 4 years old in Central Virginia on November 18, 2026. This case comes amid a larger outbreak with 186 confirmed cases in Virginia this year, 95% of which were in unvaccinated individuals. Health officials are advising unvaccinated individuals to monitor symptoms and contact healthcare providers.
Original source text (English)
The Virginia Department of Health confirmed another measles case Tuesday, the same day that an international health organization announced that the virus is no longer considered to be eliminated in the United States. The latest case involves a child who’s 4 years old or younger. The child may have exposed others at the Centra Southside Community Hospital emergency department in Farmville between 10 p.m. Saturday and 12:15 a.m. Sunday, according to a news release from the Health Department. The child also may have exposed others at the University of Virginia Medical Center in Charlottesville between 5:15 a.m. and 10:15 a.m. Monday. So far this year, Virginia has reported 186 confirmed measles cases. Unvaccinated people made up 95% of those infections, while vaccinated people made up 1%. Health officials did not know the vaccination status of the remaining 4% of people infected. In Virginia, measles cases have resulted from both international travel and household exposure, according to the Virginia Department of Health. The department’s news release did not specify whether the latest case resulted from travel or community transmission. The Health Department advises people who think they have come in contact with a positive case to report the exposure through a short survey . The survey notifies local health officials. For those who are not vaccinated, symptoms should be monitored, and contacting a healthcare provider before going to the office or emergency department is advised. Earlier this year, a measles outbreak began in Buckingham County and eventually spread to Cumberland County. The outbreak resulted in 154 confirmed cases before state health officials declared it over. The outbreak lasted 97 days. The United States first declared measles eliminated in 2000, meaning the country had gone more than 12 months without continuous transmission of the virus. International travelers could still bring measles into the country, but high vaccination rates helped prevent those cases from triggering sustained outbreaks. Between 2001 and 2019, the United States recorded 3,873 confirmed measles cases, according to a study published in the medical journal Clinical Infectious Diseases . In early 2025, a series of outbreaks began across the country and have continued throughout 2026. From the start of 2025 through Nov. 13, 2026, states reported 7,701 measles cases, according to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. On Tuesday, the Pan American Health Organization, an international public health agency that works to protect and improve health across the Americas, announced that the United States had lost its measles elimination status. Several factors have contributed to the rise in cases, including inconsistent messaging from federal health officials, declining public trust in vaccines, and cuts to public health funding and staffing. Those cuts have affected measles prevention and response efforts at the federal, state and local levels. Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s public comments about vaccines have also contributed to uncertainty surrounding vaccination. Far Southwest Virginia has some of the lowest measles vaccination rates in the state. In Scott County, 33.9% of 7-year-olds have completed the recommended measles vaccination series — the lowest rate in Virginia. The statewide average for the same population is 88.1%. Other counties across the region also fall well below the 95% vaccination threshold generally needed to prevent sustained community transmission of measles. Virginia generally requires children to provide proof of immunization , including two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine, to attend public school. The state allows certain medical and religious exemptions. Vaccination rates among 7-year-olds who have completed the measles vaccine series include: Lee County: 65.7% Wise County: 69.3% Dickenson County: 67.7% Buchanan County: 66.9% Tazewell County: 63.1% Russell County: 64% Washington County: 66.8% Grayson County: 70.3% Patrick County: 55.7% Henry County: 79.6% Related stories

Young child dies in Virginia's first measles death of 2026

News Brief
A young child in Virginia has died, marking the first measles-related death in the state for 2026. The Virginia Department of Health reported 220 measles cases, with 40 among children aged 0 to 4 and 75 among those aged 5 to 12. Health officials are investigating and advising the public to take preventive measures to avoid future tragedies.
Original source text (English)
Find us on Google 📌 America's birthday 🎂 Start the day smarter ☀️ Get the USA TODAY app U.S. Politics Sports Entertainment Life Money Travel Opinion Crossword ONLY AT USA TODAY: Newsletters For Subscribers Real Estate From the Archives USA TODAY PLAY Crossword Marvel Comics eNewspaper Magazines Investigations Video Podcasts Humankind Advertise Your Business WITNESS (True Crime) Just Curious Best-selling Booklist Top Workplaces Nomination Legals Press Releases Cars Boats Grocery Shopping Holidays OUR PORTFOLIO: Real Estate 10BEST USA TODAY Store Shopping Money Essentials WITNESS (True Crime) Southern Kitchen Home Internet Services HEALTHCARE Virginia Commonwealth University Add Topic Young child dies in Virginia's first measles death of 2026 Bill Atkinson Petersburg Progress-Index Nov. 20, 2026, 5:25 p.m. ET Hear this story Virginia has recorded the first death of the year related to measles. The Virginia Department of Health said in a statement late in the afternoon Nov. 20 a child younger than 4 years old died. The department did not say where the child lived or whether or not the child was vaccinated. "To protect the family’s privacy, VDH will not provide any further information regarding this death," the statement read. Just hours before the death was announced, VDH noted an increase in measles activity in some areas of Virginia and urged people to "be proactive in protecting themselves and their communities from measles and avoiding future tragedies." The news came three days after a young child in the same age range tested positive for measles at UVA Health University Medical Center in Charlottesville, but VDH would not say if the cases were linked. On the same day the child was reported sick in Charlottesville, the United States lost its measles-free status by the World Health Organization. Measles can cause serious complications, including pneumonia, brain inflammation, complications during pregnancy and death. Virginia cases rise above 200 this year Virginia has reported 220 measles cases in 2026, including 40 among children aged 0 to 4 and 75 among those aged 5 to 12, state health department data showed as of Nov. 19. Of those cases, 179 were in unvaccinated individuals, 39 were in people whose vaccination status was unknown and two were in vaccinated individuals. Virginia health officials have reported increased measles activity driven largely by outbreaks in central region. Buckingham County has reported 127 cases, the highest in the state, while neighboring Charlotte County has recorded 34 cases. Measles cases growing around the nation Pennsylvania, the hardest-hit US state, reported an eighth measles-related death and confirmed 1,631 measles cases, as of Nov. 20. Wisconsin has also reported two deaths, while New York state recorded its first measles-associated death, involving a child. The CDC has confirmed six measles-associated deaths so far in 2026, though it does not specify the states where the deaths were recorded. A discrepancy between state and federal death tallies arose earlier this year after the CDC, under US Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., began using National Center for Health Statistics mortality data instead of state figures. The sharp increase in US measles cases has coincided with declining immunization rates and rising vaccine hesitancy under Kennedy, who has for years spread doubts, without scientific evidence, about the safety of measles vaccines. He specializes in coverage of breaking news, crime, government, and local/state/national politics. He is an avid history buff and a lifelong Washington Commanders fan. US Loses Measles Elimination Status After 26 Years. CDC Reports 5,412 Cases And 43 Outbreaks.

News Brief
The United States has lost its measles elimination status after 26 years, with 5,412 cases and 43 new outbreaks reported in 2026. The Pan American Health Organization confirmed the decision, ending the US's public-health designation since 2000. Health authorities are investigating the sustained transmission.

By Dianna Hawryluk November 17, 2026 12:06 pm Facebook X More LinkedIn Email SMS ContributedPhotographed early in 2014 in the Philippines capital city of Manila, this baby was in a hospital with measles (rubeola). Image courtesy of James Goodson/CDC WHATCOM COUNTY, WA (MyBellinghamNow.com) – Local and regional health authorities are speaking out following the U.S. losing its measles elimination status. Losing elimination status means that a country or region has experienced uninterrupted transmission of the disease with the same genetic strain for at least 12 months according to the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO). PAHO rescinded the elimination status as the CDC reported 5,412 confirmed measles cases nationwide in 2026. Last year saw nearly half the same number of cases confirmed in the U.S. Washington had a total of 58 cases in 2026, mostly in the first half of the year. That’s up from 12 cases confirmed in 2025. Whatcom County Health and Community Services says that outbreaks of any infectious disease, such as measles, can be prevented if at least 95% of the population is vaccinated. Their most recent data shows that 87% of kindergarteners have received both doses of the MMR vaccine, which has a 97% efficacy rate. My Bellingham Now previously reported on two measles cases in Lynden last year, but no additional cases have since been reported in Whatcom County. Both Whatcom County Health and Community Services and the West Coast Health Alliance are urging people to stay up to date with MMR vaccines, especially for children. The American Academy of Pediatrics has long recommended children receive the first dose of the MMR at 12 months old before getting the second dose around age 4. (AP Photo/John Locher) Seahawks News The Seahawks are heading into their bye week on a high note. The ‘Hawks beat the Raiders 21-13 in Las Vegas yesterday to improve to 6-4 on the season. No. 1 pick Fernando Mendoza scored his first career touchdown after a Drew Lock fumble to give the Raiders an early lead. But it was all Seattle from there, as Jaxon Smith-Njigba led the way with 171 yards and a touchdown and the Seahawks’ defense racked up six sacks and 12 quarterback hits. They have the week off before traveling to face their rival 49ers in a crucial divisional matchup next week. Week 10 of the NFL season wraps up tonight when the Baltimore Ravens host the Los Angeles Chargers on Monday Night Football. Jacoby Brissett will look to help Baltimore stay in the playoff hunt in his Ravens debut at quarterback, while the Chargers are still searching for their first win of the season. Coverage begins at 4:00 and kickoff is at 5:15 on KPUG. Huskies/Cougs News The Huskies earned a crucial win over the weekend. They beat Michigan State 21-14 in East Lansing on Saturday to earn their sixth win of the season and become eligible for a bowl game. Both teams traded a pair of touchdowns before Jayden Limar ran in the winning score for UW with just two minutes left to cap off an 8-play, 78-yard drive. It came after the Huskies arrived in East Lansing at 3 a.m. that morning, just 15 hours before kickoff, due to delays from the FAA’s decision to ground all flights for several hours on Friday. Next up, they’re back at home to host 6th nationally-ranked Indiana on Saturday. Meanwhile, the Cougs thumped Colorado State 73-7 to snap their six-game skid and earn their second win of the season. Junior running back Kirby Vorhees ran for 197 yards and four touchdowns. Prep Football News The state quarterfinals are set in prep football. Four Whatcom County teams are still standing after the opening round of the state tournament over the weekend. In Class 1A, Meridian edged Seton Catholic 42-41 on the road to earn their first state football tournament win since 2018. The 9th-seeded Trojans will now travel to face top-seeded Royal at 3 p.m. on Saturday, as they look to dethrone the 6x defending state champs. Mount Baker dominated Tenino 41-0 to earn their second shutout win of the postseason. The 5th-seeded Mountaineers will now travel to face 4th-seeded Rochester at 7 p.m. on Saturday. Lynden Christian had the week off with a first-round bye, as the 3rd-seeded Lyncs now gear up to host 6th-seeded Zillah at Blaine High School on Saturday at 4:00. In Class 2A, Lynden beat Fife 42-25, as the 2nd-seeded Lions moved on to face 10th-seeded Franklin Pierce. Lynden will again host the game from Ferndale High School at 1 p.m. on Saturday. And in Class 3A, Ferndale fell to Eastside Catholic 51-14, as the Golden Eagles ended their season with a 8-3 record. Prep Volleyball News It was a successful weekend for Whatcom County’s prep volleyball teams at the state tournament. Lynden Christian shocked 6x defending state champion Chelan on Friday and went on to advance to their first state championship appearance since 2018 before falling to Cashmere. Meridian also made an impressive run and took home a 4th-place finish. In Class 2A, defending state champion Lynden got upset by Ridgefield on Saturday. The Lions ended up defeating Sehome in the 3rd place game, as the Mariners earned 4th. Meanwhile, Ferndale earned a big win as the 3A district tournament came to a close. The Golden Eagles swept Edmonds-Woodway on Saturday to book a ticket to the state tournament, which takes place this coming weekend in Yakima. Prep Girls Soccer News All three of Whatcom County’s girls soccer teams were bounced from the state tournament over the weekend. On Friday, Ferndale lost to Mercer Island in the Round of 16. On Saturday, Sehome lost to Ridgefield in the 2A quarterfinals and Lynden Christian fell to University Prep in the 1A quarterfinals. WWU Vikings News Playoff soccer is still alive at the college level in Bellingham. Western’s women’s soccer team beat Cal Poly Pomona 1-0 yesterday in their first match of the national tournament. Junior midfielder Layne Davis scored her first goal of the season to propel the win. It came against an impressive opponent, as Cal Poly Pomona won the 2024 national championship and made the final four a year ago. The 4th nationally-ranked Vikings now advance to face Concordia Irvine in the West Regional Final, which takes place in Bellingham at 3 p.m. this Friday. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

伊利诺伊州卫生局长在美国失去 麻疹 消除地位后猛烈抨击特朗普政府 - STLPR

Illinois health chief slams Trump administration after U.S. loses measles elimination status - STLPR
新闻简报伊利诺伊州卫生官员在 2026 年确认了 29 例 麻疹 病例,而密苏里州报告了 24 例。由于泛美卫生组织宣布该病毒已不间断传播一年多,美国已失去 麻疹 消除状态。这标志着与 2000 年美国中断国内 麻疹 传播 指定的逆转。伊利诺伊州公共卫生部主任 Sameer Vohra 博士将 疫苗接种 比率下降归咎于这一失败,并批评特朗普政府。CDC 指出,麻疹 具有高度传染性,附近每 10 名未接种疫苗的人中多达 9 人可能会感染该病毒。截至 11 月中旬,2026 年报告了 5,400 多例病例,凸显了 暴发 的死灰复燃。Vohra 强调,基于证据的 疫苗接种 努力几十年来一直保持消除状态,提高免疫率可以帮助美国重新获得这一地位。目前情况仍在调查中,重点是应对病毒传播的公共卫生措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
跳到主要内容 搜索查询 显示搜索 新闻报道 艺术 文化与历史 经济与商业 教育 政府、政治与问题 健康、科学与环境 法律与秩序 地铁东部报道 种族、身份与信仰 罗拉地区报道 调查 中西部新闻室 圣路易斯
地点与对象
美国,伊利诺伊州,密苏里州。
事件
伊利诺伊州卫生官员今年确诊了 29 例 麻疹 病例,密苏里州确诊了 24 例。据泛美卫生组织报告,由于病毒持续传播一年多,麻疹 失去了消除状态。
规模、影响与风险
截至 11 月中旬,美国 2026 年报告了 5,400 多例 麻疹 病例。该病毒已不间断传播一年多,导致暴发死灰复燃。由于该病毒的传染性以及导致严重疾病或死亡的可能性,该风险被明确列为高风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
伊利诺伊州卫生局长批评特朗普政府失去了 麻疹 消除状态,理由是 麻疹 比率下降。敦促提高免疫率以恢复消除状态。目前情况仍在调查中,需要采取新的公共卫生措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Illinois health chief slams Trump administration after U.S. loses measles elimination status - STLPR

News Brief
Illinois health officials confirmed 29 measles cases in 2026, while Missouri reported 24 cases. The U.S. has lost its measles elimination status, as the Pan American Health Organization declared that the virus has circulated nonstop for over a year. This marks a reversal from the 2000 designation when the U.S. interrupted domestic measles transmission. Dr. Sameer Vohra, Illinois Department of Public Health director, blamed declining vaccination rates and criticized the Trump administration for the failure. The CDC notes that measles is highly contagious, with up to 9 out of 10 unvaccinated people nearby likely to contract it. Over 5,400 cases were reported in 2026 as of mid-November, highlighting the resurgence of outbreaks. Vohra emphasized that evidence-based vaccination efforts had maintained elimination status for decades and that improving immunization rates could help the U.S. regain that standing. The situation remains under investigation, with a focus on public health measures to address the spread of the virus.
Original source text (English)

Report Public Documents & Policies Station History Station Updates & Press News Briefs Illinois health chief slams Trump administration after U.S. loses measles elimination status St. Louis Public Radio | By Rob Edwards Published Nov. 17, 2026 at 12:44 p.m. CST Facebook Twitter LinkedIn Email U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention Measles is a highly contagious virus. According to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, if one person is infected, up to 9 out of 10 people nearby will also be infected unless they are protected. Illinois’ top health official is criticizing the Trump administration following an international health organization’s decision to strip the United States of its measles elimination status. The Pan American Health Organization, a regional branch of the World Health Organization, announced Tuesday that measles is no longer eliminated in the U.S. because the virus has circulated in the country nonstop for more than a year. The designation had been held by the United States since 2000, when health officials determined the country had interrupted the continuous domestic transmission of measles. In a statement responding to the announcement, Dr. Sameer Vohra, Illinois Department of Public Health director, blamed declining national vaccination rates for the reversal and sharply criticized the Trump administration. “The loss of measles elimination status in the U.S. is the direct result of declining national vaccination rates,” Vohra said. “When measles/mumps/rubella (MMR) vaccination rates are high, communities experience far fewer outbreaks and, as a result, a lower likelihood of serious illness or death. This is a serious failure of the Trump administration.” Vohra said the country maintained its elimination status for decades because of evidence-based vaccination efforts and argued that renewed efforts to improve immunization rates could help the U.S. regain that standing. “The U.S. maintained measles elimination status for decades because evidence-based science shows immunization works, and a commitment to improving vaccination rates can get us back to a place where measles cases are nearly non-existent,” he said. The United States has experienced a resurgence of measles outbreaks over the past two years, with more than 5,400 cases reported in 2026 as of mid-November. Illinois health officials have confirmed 29 cases of the virus this year. Missouri health officials have confirmed 24 cases of measles so far in 2026. Have a question or comment about this story? 随着美国失去消除状态,俄亥俄州 麻疹 病例较 2025 年增加 700% - 哥伦布快报

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ohio measles cases up 700% from 2025 as US loses elimination status - The Columbus Dispatch

News Brief
Ohio has seen a significant increase in measles cases, with a 700% rise from 2025 to 358 cases in 28 counties this year. The United States has lost its measles elimination status, with measles cases reaching a 35-year high. Health officials emphasize the importance of vaccination to prevent further spread.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Business Opinion Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals HEALTHCARE Ohio measles cases up 700% from 2025 as US loses elimination status Chad Murphy James Powel Mary Walrath-Holdridge USA TODAY NETWORK Nov. 20, 2026, 12:50 p.m. ET Ohio is experiencing its third measles outbreak of 2026, with cases increasing by 82% in the past week. The state has reported 358 cases in 28 counties this year, a nearly 700% increase from all of 2025. Nationally, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, and the United States has lost its measles elimination status. Health officials state the vast majority of cases are among unvaccinated individuals and urge vaccination as the best protection. As another person may have spread measles across five locations in Western Ohio, two more counties joined the list of confirmed cases as the state's third measles outbreak of 2026 continues. Ohio added 31 new cases in the past week, a 82% increase from the 17 new cases reported the week before, the Ohio Department of Health reports. ODH is reporting 358 cases in 28 of 88 Ohio counties so far this year, according to its summary of infectious diseases dashboard, updated Nov. 12. That number is nearly seven times (696.5%) more than the 45 cases reported in all of 2025. Paulding and Jackson counties joined the list this week with one case each. Geauga and Wayne counties had the most new cases, with nine and eight each, respectively. Nationally, the United States has officially lost its measles-free status. The disease had been declared eradicated from the US in 2000 in what the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention called a "historic" accomplishment. Measles cases nationwide have hit a 35-year high. As of Nov. 13, Johns Hopkins University counted 5,739 measles cases in the U.S. in 2026, and as of Nov. 5, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported 5,182 confirmed measles cases in 45 states plus Washington, D.C. – more than double last year's total of 2,289, USA TODAY reports. That's the highest annual total since 1991. The vast majority of cases in Ohio are among unvaccinated individuals, per ODH. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Measles growing in Ohio. These counties report cases in 2026 Ohio counties reporting measles cases since Jan. 1, 2026, include: Ashland: 8 Ashtabula: 58 (five new cases) Cuyahoga: 3 Franklin: 8 Gallia: 3 Geauga: 65 (nine new cases) Hancock: 1 Harrison: 18 Holmes: 14 (four new cases) Jackson: 1 (one new case; first time on the list) Knox: 5 Mahoning: 2 (one new case) Medina: 28 Meigs: 4 Mercer: 4 Morgan: 3 Morrow: 13 Ottawa: 1 Paulding: 1 (one new case; first time on the list) Pike: 26 (two new cases) Portage: 1 Stark: 1 Trumbull: 16 (one new case) Tuscarawas: 15 Van Wert: 3 Vinton: 1 Wayne: 54 (eight new cases) Wood: 1 The total number of new cases in the by-county list this week totals 32. That differs from overall total of new cases because one new measles case last week was credited to an unknown county. The case count for the year reflects previous outbreaks in Cuyahoga County in January and Franklin County in February. The ages of those with confirmed cases range from 15 months to 20 years old, according to various health departments. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with one case in 2023, seven in 2024 and 45 cases in 2025. Weekly Ohio measles cases by the numbers Total cases in Ohio this year have continued climbing, as reported by ODH: 327 cases in 26 counties on Nov. 12 310 cases in 25 counties on Nov. 5 296 cases in 24 counties on Oct. 29 260 cases in 24 counties on Oct. 22 252 cases in 23 counties on Oct. 15 228 cases in 21 counties on Oct. 8 206 cases in 20 counties on Oct. 1 196 cases in 16 counties on Sept. 24 180 cases in 15 counties on Sept. 17 133 cases in 15 counties on Sept. 10 122 cases in 14 counties on Sept. 3 107 cases in 12 counties on Aug. 27 99 cases in 12 counties Aug. 20 69 cases in 11 counties on Aug. 13 45 cases in 10 counties on Aug. 6 Measles kills six in Pennsylvania; infant dies of measles in Wisconsin Pennsylvania confirmed its sixth measles-related death of the year on Nov. 4, while New York announced its first measles death in 30 years on Nov. 2, according to USA TODAY. Wisconsin also reported a grim milestone on Nov. 5, with health officials saying that its first measles death since 1990 was that of an infant. Measles cases on the rise as Ohio vaccination rates fluctuate in state, U.S. Vaccination rates for measles have declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated. Most people are then protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–20 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–25 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. Ohio's vaccination rate for the 2025-26 school year slipped well below the herd immunity threshold, making it susceptible to outbreaks. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventive measures, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of a 2025 Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. Kennedy is now encouraging the measles vaccine. Trump signs executive order to separate MMR vaccine President Donald Trump in August 2026 signed an executive order to split the childhood measles, mumps and rubella vaccine into three shots and delivering them at separate medical visits as well as "calling for more vaccine flexibility and research" and fewer childhood vaccinations, according to USA TODAY. "You have the MMR, we want it in three separate vaccinations, given at separate times. Together there could be a possibility they're quite lethal and separately, it looks like they are not at all lethal, but just very effective," said Trump from the Oval Office as he signed the order. But the MMR vaccine is not lethal, and medical research shows no health benefit or reduced risk from splitting it. In contrast, separating the shots increases injection pain, leaves children unprotected longer, and lowers overall vaccination rates, according to experts. The MMR vaccine has been in existence since 1971, according to the World Health Organization. Measles cases hit 35-year high in United States, country loses 'eliminated' status The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to USA TODAY, citing the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. The United States has officially lost its measles-free status three decades after the "historic public health achievement" that eliminated the highly infectious disease from the country, the Pan American Health Organization, the part of the World Health Organization that tracks infectious disease in the Americas, announced Nov. 17. Defined by the WHO as freedom from the continuous spread of a disease for at least 12 months, the United States achieved measles elimination status in 2000 in what the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention called a "historic" accomplishment. Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, director of PAHO, pointed to gaps in vaccination coverage as a significant culprit. Can you get measles if vaccinated? Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but they can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before through four days after a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. USA TODAY reporters James Powel and Mary Walrath-Holdridge contributed to this report. Spinal Stenosis Is Not From Tight Muscles. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Find out whether you need a measles booster shot

News Brief
The article discusses whether adults need measles booster shots in light of recent measles outbreaks in the U.S. Experts generally advise that most people are protected without boosters, especially those vaccinated before 1968 or those who had measles as children. However, certain groups like healthcare workers born before 1957 and those who received an inactivated vaccine between 1963 and 1967 may benefit from boosters. The guidance is preliminary, and individuals should consult their doctors for personalized advice.

(Mary Conlon / Associated Press) By Laura Ungar Nov. 20, 2026 8 AM PT 5 min Click here to listen to this article Share via Close extra sharing options Email Facebook X LinkedIn Threads Reddit WhatsApp Copy Link URL Copied! Print 0:00 0:00 1x This is read by an automated voice. Please report any issues or inconsistencies here. Now that measles is officially back in the U.S., some Americans are asking: Do adults need a booster shot? Generally, experts say, the answer is no. “Most people are protected without getting a booster shot” because they’ve either been vaccinated or had measles as kids, said Dr. Richard Martinello at Yale School of Medicine. The U.S., Mexico and Bolivia lost their measles-free status this week after stubborn outbreaks erased decades of progress. Last year, Canada lost its measles elimination designation. Advertisement Doctors say getting two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine is the best way to protect against one of the most contagious viruses in the world. The vaccine usually protects people for life, as does having had the disease. Americans born before 1957 are considered to have “presumptive evidence” of immunity because almost everyone back then got measles during childhood. For some people, boosters may be a good idea But doctors say certain people should consider getting boosters. One group is healthcare workers born before 1957 without proof of immunity. They’re more likely than the general public to come in contact with people who have measles, so they may get it or even spread it to vulnerable patients. Adults vaccinated between 1963 and 1967 also might want to think about boosters. During those years, some children received an inactivated vaccine that uses a killed version of the virus, which was less effective than the current vaccine containing live, weakened virus. Doctors said people who know they got the inactivated vaccine or aren’t sure should get at least one dose of the live MMR vaccine. One dose is 93% effective at preventing measles, while two doses is 97% protective. Until 1989, only a single dose was standard. Now the recommendation is for adults to have at least one documented dose of MMR. Two doses is recommended for certain people in high-risk groups who don’t have other evidence of immunity. Advertisement “People who have had no shots should go get vaccinated, if they weren’t born before 1957,” said Dr. Joshua Sharfstein at Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health. “If they have had only one shot, they may want to consider whether they’re in a risk group for a second shot.” That risk group, he said, includes people in school, healthcare workers, international travelers or people living with family members who have compromised immune systems. In places with active measles outbreaks, health officials sometimes recommend that everyone have two shots, Sharfstein said. For people who have had only one shot, especially in an area where measles is circulating, “it’s always a good idea to talk with your physician about whether another shot is needed,” Martinello said. Tracking down your vaccination status But what if you don’t know how many shots you received or even if you’ve been vaccinated? The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention suggests asking your parents or doctor, checking with your high school or college health services or reaching out to previous employers that required immunizations. You can also try contacting your state’s health department; some states have registries that include adult vaccines. If you’re still unsure about your vaccination status, you can talk with your doctor about an MMR immunity blood test that can show whether you have antibodies against measles. But Martinello said this isn’t an ideal measure of how safe you are from the virus. Though the test is accurate, he said, scientists don’t know how to link the result perfectly with the level of protection someone might have. Regardless, doctors said getting a booster just to be safe won’t harm you unless you’re in a group that is not supposed to get the vaccine at all, such as pregnant women. Advertisement Now that measles is circulating in the U.S., doctors said it’s crucial to take measures to protect yourself. “This is a moment to not just check for yourself, but also encourage others to make sure that they are adequately vaccinated,” Sharfstein said. “Too many people took measles for granted ... or just didn’t think about measles. But now it’s something to think about.” Ungar writes for the Associated Press. Measles Cases Among Pregnant Women Rise Sharply, Alarming Doctors - KFBK
新闻简报2026年,美国,特别是宾夕法尼亚州孕妇中的麻疹病例数急剧上升至112例确诊病例。这导致先天性 麻疹 病例增加,使母亲和新生儿面临严重风险。CDC 警告说,美国可能很快就会失去自 2000 年以来一直保持的 麻疹 自由状态。健康专家敦促加强孕前咨询和 麻疹,以防止进一步传播。

时间
立即播放 登录注册 全国新闻 孕妇 麻疹 病例急剧上升,令医生震惊 作者:iHeartRadio 2026 年 11 月 16 日 照片:chameleonseye / iStock 社论 / Getty Images 2026 年将有 100 多名孕妇被确认患有 麻疹,导致出生时患有先天性心脏病的婴儿数量增加,美国各地的医生都对此表示担忧麻疹 并将母亲及其新生儿置于严重危险之中。
地点与对象
美国,重点关注宾夕法尼亚州。
事件
孕妇麻疹病例急剧增加,已报告确诊病例112例,导致先天性麻疹病例增加,使母亲和新生儿面临严重风险。该峰值与 疫苗接种 费率下降有关。
规模、影响与风险
从 2025 年初到 9 月底,美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 收到了 13 例先天性 麻疹 病例的报告,近期病例数激增与 麻疹 发病率下降密切相关。CDC 警告说,美国可能很快就会失去自 2000 年以来一直保持的 麻疹 免接种状态。MMR 麻疹 不能在怀孕期间接种,但暴露于 麻疹 的未接种疫苗的孕妇可以接受免疫球蛋白抗体,以帮助降低感染和并发症的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
疾病预防控制中心和健康专家敦促计划怀孕的人以及家庭成员和其他可能与孕妇或新生儿密切接触的人增加孕前咨询和 疫苗接种。目前情况正在调查中,暴发 正在进行,并且需要提高 疫苗接种 费率以防止进一步传播。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles Cases Among Pregnant Women Rise Sharply, Alarming Doctors - KFBK

News Brief
In 2026, the number of measles cases among pregnant women in the United States, particularly in Pennsylvania, has risen sharply to 112 confirmed cases. This has led to a rise in congenital measles cases, putting both mothers and newborns at serious risk. The CDC warns that the U.S. could soon lose its measles-free status, which it has held since 2000. Health experts are urging increased preconception counseling and vaccination to prevent further spread.
Original source text (English)
Play Now Log InSign Up National News Measles Cases Among Pregnant Women Rise Sharply, Alarming Doctors By iHeartRadio Nov 16, 2026 Photo: chameleonseye / iStock Editorial / Getty Images Doctors across the United States are raising concerns as more than 100 pregnant women have been confirmed to have measles in 2026, leading to a rise in babies born with congenital measles and putting both mothers and their newborns at serious risk. According to preliminary figures published in a medical journal, 13 cases of congenital measles were reported to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) from the start of 2025 through the end of September, a phenomenon not seen in decades. Congenital measles occurs when a pregnant woman passes the virus to her fetus, which can cause severe complications or even be fatal for newborns. The recent spike is closely linked to declining vaccination rates, prompting experts to warn that the U.S. could soon lose the measles-free status it has held since 2000. The CDC's alert, released in partnership with NEJM Evidence, highlighted 112 confirmed cases of measles during pregnancy, most of which were among women who had not been vaccinated or whose vaccination status was unknown. Pennsylvania experienced a record 97 confirmed measles cases among pregnant women in 2026, the highest in decades. Pennsylvania’s Department of Health has issued advisories to healthcare providers and the public, underlining the risks of both maternal complications—such as pneumonia, pregnancy loss, and premature delivery—and the potential for congenital measles in newborns. Dr. Sharee Livingston, an OBGYN in Central Pennsylvania, told ABC News, "You have to watch the mother fetus dyad so closely because of the risk of fetal death." She described the situation as "a very challenging position to be in" for all involved. Congenital measles may result in symptoms like fever, rash, jaundice, and severe complications such as pneumonia, encephalitis, and subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a rare brain disorder that can be fatal, sometimes developing more quickly in these infants. The CDC and health experts stress that the best prevention is for women to ensure they are vaccinated with the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine before becoming pregnant. The MMR vaccine cannot be given during pregnancy, but unvaccinated pregnant women exposed to measles can receive immunoglobulin antibodies to help reduce the risk of infection and complications. National MMR vaccination coverage for kindergarteners was 92.4% in the 2025–26 school year, but experts warn outbreaks are more likely when community rates fall below 95%. Many communities remain under this threshold. As outbreaks continue, experts and officials urge increased preconception counseling and vaccination for those planning pregnancy, as well as for family members and others who may be in close contact with pregnant women or newborns.
